#252. Borussia Dortmund bows to fan pressure and end Rheinmetall deal

Borussia Dortmund has reluctantly ended its association with a major sponsor after long-term fan protests. They are not the first Bundesliga club to u-turn, as commercial interests clash with politics and fan culture.

Two years of fan pressure and criticism has forced Borussia Dortmund to drop a sponsorship deal with Germany's largest arms manufacturer, Rheinmetall.

The Bundesliga club will not renew its deal with Rheinmetall beyond its expiry in June 2027, at the end of this season. Dortmund chairman Carsten Cramer confirmed the news on the club's website ahead of its AGM on Sunday, which may have proved a potential flashpoint. He acknowledged that "our partnership has been controversial from the outset" but lamented the loss of income.

"It reduces our financial room for manoeuvre. We will have to reprioritize planned investments and carefully examine how we can find viable solutions."

Though he welcomed the news, one of the leaders of the fan revolt against the partnership, Michael Schulze von Glaser, warned this is likely not the end of the road.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I think in the end it was a victory for the fans. But I'm also a bit skeptical about  what the future will bring us," the Dortmund fan and former head of the German Peace Society told DW. "Football fans in Germany always must be skeptical and always must watch what the club is doing.”

Many Dortmund fans have opposed the deal — which has seen Rheinmetall's logo on advertising boards and other surfaces at locations such as Dortmund's 83,000-capacity Westfalenstadion — from its beginnings in 2024. It was estimated to be worth between €7 ($7.68 million) and €9 million per year.

At the club's AGM that year, 556 of 855 members present voted against the deal in a non-binding vote. There were 247 votes in favour, with 52 abstentions. This, said Cramer, informed the eventual outcome.

Rheinmetall sells arms mostly to Germany, Ukraine and NATO but fans and critics feel that platforming any arms manufacturer goes against the club's statement of its core values. In that, Dortmund states its desire for "a club and a society that operate free of racism, anti-Semitism, LGBTQIA+ hostility, sexism, violence and discrimination."

To play this audio please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 audio

It has always been clear, said Schulze von Glaser, that this deal did not chime with that.

"In the end, they kill people. They are for war," he said of Rheinmetall. "The slogan of Borussia Dortmund is true love (Echte Liebe). But for me and many others, our love ends there…. I know some fans who have put fan merchandise in the trash because they were so shocked.”

In the Q and A on the club's website, Cramer said he was still a "personal advocate" of the deal but that Dortmund's commitment to the 50+1 model, which means German clubs are majority controlled by the members, meant they had to listen to fan voices. But he also warned that Rheinmetall must be an "absolute exception and an isolated case."

That set off some alarm bells for Schulze von Glaser.

"It's a bit concerning that there are constantly these attacks on professional football — a new sponsorship, investment deals," he said.

Indeed, Dortmund's climbdown is not the first of its kind in Germany's top flight. Last year, after fan and media pressure, Bayern Munich ended its commercial deal with the tourism arm of the Rwandan government before its conclusion.

Fans had objected to the promotion of Rwanda, which many felt sought to divert attention from its poor human rights record and actions in the war with DR Congo, through sporting partnerships. Two years earlier, Bayern withdrew from a similarly controversial deal with Qatar Airways.

Elsewhere in the league, Werder Bremen fans objected to their long time shirt sponsor, Wiesenhof. Many supporters took issue with the factory farming practices  of Germany's largest poultry producer. Though it was not given as a direct reason for the end of a shirt sponsorship deal in 2023, it likely played a part.

Union Berlin fans have also made their voices heard. When the club signed a new shirt-sponsorship deal with Luxembourg real estate company, Aroundtown, upon promotion to the top flight in 2019, many fans were frustrated at the tie up in a city where rent prices were skyrocketing.

"It's a problem for a lot of people," fan Sven Dobberstein told DW at the time.

"We have to try to find a way between commercialization and what we have at the moment. It isn't easy."

Striking that balance can often be a source of tension for Bundesliga clubs. 50+1 ensures fans have a voice, preventing rich owners, or nation states, from taking control as they do in England's lucrative Premier League or in France or Spain. Indeed fan protests have also stopped the league signing a €1billion private equity partner and seen Monday night matches scrapped. For Schulze von Glaser, this needs to be protected.

"In Germany, the fan culture has an impact," he said.

”If there is something bad or terrible happening in your club and in the Bundesliga, there are the organized fans and they can have an impact if they want, if they stand together and, yes, they can stop the Rheinmetall deal."

You can edit the text, music, stickers, and drawings in your Stories for up to 15 minutes after sharing.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Instagram’s latest update will allow you to edit your Stories without losing your views, likes, or replies. You can now edit the text, music, stickers, and drawings for up to 15 minutes after sharing your Story, Instagram head Adam Mosseri has announced.

Reverse engineer Alessandro Paluzzi first spotted Instagram working on this feature in April, but now it’s official. Previously, you had to delete and repost your Story to fix a typo or add a song that you forgot to include.

You can edit your post by opening your Story and selecting the three-dot menu in the bottom-right corner. From there, tap “Edit Story” to start making changes.

In addition to editable Stories, Instagram announced that it’s launching new fonts, colors, and shapes for stickers, as well as presenting them on sticker sheets that are supposed to be easier to browse. The platform is also making changes to the way you select multiple photos and adding new blur, colors, and gradient background options.

#249. Sam Altman warns against a future where AI, not humans, calls the shots

OpenAI co-founder and CEO Sam Altman believes that people should stay in charge of the future when it comes to how AI technology impacts the world and everyone’s lives. Speaking at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the AI executive referred to discussions in Silicon Valley about whether there will be a gradual handover to AI — meaning AI will eventually become the decision-making authority across every major industry and institution.

“One thing we’ve been talking a lot about recently is there’s been this thing in Silicon Valley that seems almost ridiculous to have to fight against, but we both believe very deeply that people need to stay in control of the future, and there’s increasing talk about, well, there is either going to be or there should be this sort of gradual handover to AI. And I — we both — agree that that’s a terrible idea,” said Altman, who was sharing the stage with OpenAI co-founder and president Greg Brockman.

The hand-off theory stems from the idea that as AI models become increasingly more powerful, they will learn to improve themselves — a concept known as “recursive self-improvement” or RSI. As existing AI technology builds newer, smarter generations, those will eventually become a superintelligence that humans can no longer control, the belief goes.

Altman explained that he and Brockman only disagree over how to best accomplish the goal of keeping humans in control, or what sort of challenges we should expect to face along the way. However, Altman characterized this as more of a debate between two people who share the same larger views.

“The question of how to actually prevent [handover] from happening — how to actually ensure that we will, given all of the various pressures and given what other companies may try to do — that we are going to actually ensure that people stay in control of the future in a very deep way, and we don’t have this either accidental or intentional handover — I think we can both agree on that,” Altman said.

Brockman added that, as the models improve, there’s an increasing responsibility for alignment, safety, and security, which may require changes in how things are currently done.

“Monitoring is a good example of something where I think we are going to need new … technology,” he said.

Today, AI researchers monitor the model’s chain of thought, where the AI writes out what it’s thinking as it works through problems and performs tasks. But in Brockman’s view, that may not always be the best way to monitor more advanced AI that’s being built now.

“As the models have gotten more capable, they can do more within just the forward path without having to actually write out that thinking, which means we need a new approach to monitoring,” Brockman said. “We’re going to need to deliver that, and I think that there will be points where we need to really think about, as the capabilities get to a certain point, chain-of-thought monitorability is degraded,” he continued, adding that all the problems were technical ones to be solved.

OpenAI had committed to increased chain-of-thought monitoring after the incident where an OpenAI model escaped containment and hacked into the AI model repository Hugging Face. In Wednesday’s interview, Brockman noted that the company told 25% of its production engineers to stop building things and work on securing the AI technology after the hack.

But despite the high-profile breach, he argued that forward progress was still critical to fight bad actors.

“I think to realize that if you look to the level of capabilities of models in the world, and you see cyber models that are coming from that are now … in the hands of threat actors everywhere, that I think that it’s really important that defenders use the most capable models to defend,” he said.

This screen is one the best we’ve tested, and the lower price makes it more competitive with other OLED televisions.

You can also save $800 on the 55-inch model, which is typically $2,599.99 (Amazon, Best Buy, Sony), but now costs just under $1,800.

The connectivity includes two HDMI 2.1 (one with eARC), and two HDMI 2.0 ports, giving you plenty of inputs for consoles and a Blu-ray player, among other gadgets. We spotted some color fringing of certain colors in dark scenes, and lamented the lack of a but it’s an otherwise excellent screen we can easily recommend.

#247. Study finds slightly higher risk of autism diagnosis in areas with more lithium in drinking water, but experts say more research is needed

A new study found a moderately higher risk of autism spectrum disorder in children born to pregnant people exposed to tap water with higher levels of lithium, but experts caution that this association does not show a direct link between the two.

About 1 in 36 children in the US is diagnosed with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) each year, according to data from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Scientists still don’t know the exact cause of autism, a developmental disorder. Genetics may be a factor, but some have been looking at potential environmental causes, too.

Autism diagnosis has become more common, but pandemic disrupted early detection, CDC says

Cases may be on the rise, but that is also unclear. One study published this year on cases in the New York-New Jersey area found that autism diagnosis rates tripled among certain age groups between 2000 and 2016. A 2021 report found similar increases in cases, but the CDC says the increased number of cases is most likely linked to more doctors screening for the condition.

Lithium is an alkali metal that can be found naturally in some food and ground water. It’s used in batteries, grease and air conditioners, as well as in the treatment of bipolar disorder and some blood disorders. Its levels in US drinking water are not regulated, according to the US Geological Survey.

A new study, published Monday in the journal JAMA Pediatrics, found a small association between lithium and autism diagnosis in Denmark, where the researchers say the level of lithium in drinking water is similar to that in American water systems.

The researchers checked a database of people with psychiatric disorders for children born between 2000 and 2013 to find information on 8,842 cases of ASD and 43,864 participants who did not have ASD. They then measured the concentration of lithium in 151 public waterworks that served more than half of the Danish population and mapped out where pregnant people lived in relation.

For many neurodiverse people, 'The Sims' has been a lifelong comfort

As lithium levels in water increased, there was a modest increased risk of an ASD diagnosis. Specifically, compared with people at the lowest exposure level, those who had the second and third highest exposure during pregnancy had a 24% to 26% higher risk of ASD diagnosed in children. The group with the highest exposure had a 46% higher risk than those at the lowest level of exposure.

The researchers could not tell how much water the pregnant people drank, but they picked Denmark in part because residents there consume some of the lowest amounts of bottled water in Europe.

Experts say it’s important to note that the research can’t show that lithium exposure leads directly to an autism diagnosis.

Further study is required, said study co-author Dr. Beate Ritz, a professor of neurology in the David Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA, and a professor of epidemiology and environmental health at the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health.

“Any drinking water contaminants that may affect the developing human brain deserve intense scrutiny,” Ritz said in a news release. She added that the research would need to be replicated in other countries to look for a similar connection.

The implications of the findings are complex as far as public health policy is concerned, according to an editorial published alongside the study. Lithium levels in water, at concentrations that the study associated with a potential ASD risk, have also been linked with health benefits such as lower rates of hospitalization for psychiatric disorders and suicide.

“If all these of associations are valid, the wisdom of Solomon will be required to develop guidelines for lithium in drinking water that are maximally protective of the entire population,” wrote Dr. David C. Bellinger, a professor of neurology and psychology at Harvard Medical School. “Until the basic biology of ASD is better understood, it will be difficult to distinguish causal from spurious associations.”

'Heartbreaking' delays in autism diagnosis and treatment got even worse during pandemic

Dr. Max Wiznitzer, director of the Rainbow Autism Center at University Hospitals Rainbow Babies and Children’s Hospital in Cleveland, points to other research on the effects of lithium on pregnant people who take it for mental health disorders. Those studies – which look at people exposed to much higher levels than are found in drinking water – do not show a connection with autism spectrum disorder.

“It’s an interesting association, but causation is definitely not proven,” said Wiznitzer, who was not involved in the new research. “We have to see if there’s a viable and biologically plausible mechanism by which a small amount of lithium in the water supply can somehow do this, yet pharmacologic dosing of lithium in women with bipolar disorder has not been reported to be causing increased risk of ASD.”

Other studies have also suggested connections between ASD and environmental exposures to things like pesticides, air pollution and phthalates. But none of them points to any of these factors as a direct cause of the disorder.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

A link between environmental exposure and ASD is hard to prove, Wiznitzer said. With research showing that increased exposure to air pollution raises the risk of giving birth to a child with ASD, for example, he often wonders whether pollution is the determining factor or if it’s just the populations who live in more polluted areas.

“There’s a lot of speculation about about environmental factors, but how many of them are truly causally associated?” Wiznitzer said. “We are bombarded with a variety of environmental stressors in our everyday lives. We have to figure out how to basically safely navigate them, and this is probably not one that’s high on our list.”

Scan the QR code to download the CNN app from the Apple Store.

#246. Nvidia’s rise, visualized

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

What’s next for Nvidia? Whatever doesn’t rock the mind-bogglingly profitable boat.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

What’s next for Nvidia? Whatever doesn’t rock the mind-bogglingly profitable boat.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If someone handed you $8,300 every second, what would you do? That’s how much pure profit Nvidia now makes. By the time you finish reading this paragraph, Nvidia will likely have cleared $45,000 and change.

It’s easy to look at Nvidia, a company that just lost its visionary founder CEO due to a series of scandals, and think that the company and maybe the entire AI industry are in terrible trouble. Perhaps so?

But I want you to understand: Nvidia isn’t your average semi-successful company that might tempt a new CEO to fix things that are broken. It is a company so staggeringly, mind-bogglingly successful it’ll do everything in its power not to rock the boat. There is so much money at stake.

It’s so much money that when I heard about Jensen Huang’s resignation, my first thought was: I need a way to visualize this jaw-dropping amount of money for you. I want to show you that Nvidia has risen so far, so fast, that rival chipmakers AMD, Intel, and Qualcomm barely qualify as competition now.

If you’re on a desktop OS, you could mouse over each dot in our chart above to see the staggering sums. But we built another way to visualize the rise of Nvidia I like even better. It’s a horizontal bar graph that not only puts Nvidia’s revenue growth in perspective, but shows you its monster profits right alongside them — plus the world events that made Nvidia a star.

TL;DR: The crypto boom and the great GPU shortage have nothing on the AI boom. Those earlier periods saw Nvidia’s profits briefly peak at $1.24 billion and $3 billion a quarter respectively, while the rise of data centers has driven them to $65 billion per quarter with no sign of stopping.

Yes, Jensen may have set a trillion dollars of Nvidia’s market cap on fire — from around $5 trillion at the start of November to “only” $3.6 trillion now — but the smoldering company is raking in $700 million in profit a day on average.

Put another way: Nvidia could give a $1 million bonus to every one of its 42,000 employees, every three months, without running out of cash. Or, you know, Nvidia could end world hunger or provide clean water for everyone, now that it’s making over $200 billion in pure profit a year.

Maybe that’d make people feel differently about data centers?

But even if we stick to self-serving capitalism, you’re probably still a happy investor if you bought Nvidia a decade ago. A $1,000 investment 10 years ago is now worth over $67,000. You’d have over $1 million if you invested $15,000 back then.

And make no mistake, Nvidia is sticking to capitalism. Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress just promised to return as much money to investors as possible on the company’s November 17th earnings call. “Going forward, we intend to return all excess free cash flow net of strategic uses to shareholders,” she announced. “All” is a lot more than the 50 percent baseline that Jensen promised five months ago.

Nvidia isn’t the only company whose fortunes have risen this dramatically. RAMageddon revealed that just three companies — Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron — controlled nearly 90 percent of the entire global DRAM market, and those three have repeatedly signaled that they’d prefer to celebrate record profits instead of ramping up production too fast.

But while their revenues and profits have been skyrocketing even faster over the past year or so, even Big RAM hasn’t caught the AI wave quite the same way as Nvidia. Samsung, of course, doesn’t just make memory — its revenue numbers include phones, tablets, TVs, home appliances, chips, displays, camera sensors, audio, networking equipment, medical equipment, and more.

In the most recent quarter, Micron made $37.7 billion in profit, SK Hynix made $29.8 billion, and Samsung came in at around $55.3 billion, but they’re all shy of Nvidia’s $65.3 billion. Historically, RAM has also been a bit more cyclical: Both Micron and SK Hynix actually lost money in 2023 because they produced too much memory.

On its latest earnings call, the first since cofounder Jensen Huang’s resignation, interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress reinforced the idea that Nvidia expects the good times to keep on rolling.

Asked how things might change under new management, she suggested that they wouldn’t: “From a business perspective, it’s business as usual,” she said. She added that the company has secured the backing of customers and suppliers including TSMC, the all-important firm that actually fabricates Nvidia’s chips.

“Our momentum is real, our demand is accelerating, our supply is growing. Our customers and partners are with us, and employees are committed,” she claimed. “We’ve got this.”

Nvidia claims it’ll rake in even more money next quarter, with projected revenue of $130 billion. Profit may be proportionally smaller since gross margins are estimated to drop to 71–72 percent (compared to nearly 75 percent), but Kress said Nvidia intends to get them up as high as 73 percent next year.

In other words: This astounding gravy train isn’t going anywhere. At least that’s the plan.

Stevie Bonifield contributed to this story.

#245. David Ellison says HBO will air critical Elon Musk documentary despite his $1B investment in Skydance

Skydance CEO David Ellison says HBO will air Alex Gibney’s documentary about Elon Musk, even after news broke that the SpaceX founder had invested $1 billion in the newly formed company following its Warner Bro. Discovery and Paramount merger.

Following news of that investment, questions emerged over how Musk’s sizable stake in the company might affect Skydance’s plans to distribute a documentary that is highly critical of him.

When asked by The New York Times editor and columnist Andrew Ross Sorkin whether HBO would still distribute the documentary even with Musk’s stake in its parent company, Ellison said, “We have a pay-one deal with A24, who produced that movie. Of course, we’re going to honor the deal.”

He went on to say, “We believe in independence. We don’t believe in censorship of any kind.”

Co-CEOs David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz are tasked with bringing together two major streaming platforms, Paramount+ and HBO Max, along with a portfolio of networks, including CBS, CNN, MTV, TBS, Comedy Central, and Food Network, under a single corporate umbrella.

#244. US House Democrats nominate Hakeem Jeffries for speaker

Jeffries is set to be the first Black speaker of the US House of Representatives when Democrats take control of the chamber next year. He will be second in line to the presidency.

Democrats in the US House of Representatives on Wednesday nominated Hakeem Jeffries to be the next speaker.

Jeffries, who represents a district in New York, would be the first Black person to fill the role. He received 251 out of 265 ballots cast in a closed-door, secret vote.

"Thank you House Democrats. Together, we will deliver bold change," Jeffries posted on X after the vote. "And make a big difference for the American people!"

Some progressive Democrats voted against Jeffries' speakership, with the congressman considered by some to be in the moderate wing of the party.

The entire House will do a roll call vote on January 3, 2027, to elect a speaker when the new Congress convenes. As Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in the midterm elections, claiming at least 257 of 435 seats in the lower chamber of US Congress, the 56-year-old Jeffries is all but certain to get the position.

In a rebuke of US President Donald Trump's Republican Party, Democrats also won 56 seats and control of the Senate, the upper house of US Congress.

Jeffries will steer Democratic priorities in the House, such as possible legislation and investigations of the Trump administration. He will also be second in line to the presidency after Vice President JD Vance.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Following the vote, seven of the 11 Democrats who voted against Jeffries issued a joint statement.

"Whether on Medicare for All, stopping ICE terror, getting money out of politics, or ending the genocide in Gaza, our constituents sent us to Congress with a mandate on these issues," Representatives-elect Darializa Avila Chevalier, Adam Hamawy, Frederick Haynes, Melat Kiros, Donavan McKinney, Chris Rabb and Claire Valdez wrote.

"We have worked to secure commitments from Leader Jeffries in good-faith conversations. While we have seen meaningful progress, there is still work to be done to ensure that the direction our party takes this coming Congress reflects what our constituents are demanding of leadership," they added. "Those conversations are ongoing. As such, our vote in caucus today was no."

Under Jeffries, Massachusetts Representative Katherine Clark will serve as female majority leader, and Pete Aguilar of California will serve as majority whip. Clark will be the first woman to serve in that position, with Aguilar the first Latino to fill his elected role in the House.

For the Republican House delegation in the new Congress, Jim Jordan, a representative of Ohio, will be the next House minority leader. Jordan is a staunch Trump supporter.

In the Senate, Chuck Schumer of New York is the frontrunner to lead Democrats in the upper chamber, with Maryland Senator Chris Van Hollen also in the running.

Republican South Dakota Senator John Thune, who currently serves as Senate majority leader, is expected to be minority leader next year.

#243. Syrian refugee elected mayor of German town, years after fleeing war

A Syrian who arrived in Germany as a refugee in 2015 has won a mayoral election in the southwestern German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Ryyan Alshebl, who left his hometown of As Suwayda in Syria eight years ago, ran as an independent in the municipality of Ostelsheim. He won 55.41% of the votes on Sunday, beating two German candidates, Marco Strauss and Mathias Fey.

Locals cheered the 29-year-old when he welcomed his win, a victory he described as “sensational,” German local broadcaster SWR reported Monday.

“Today, Ostelsheim sent an example for broad-mindedness and cosmopolitanism for the whole of Germany,” he said, according to to German public broadcaster ZDF. “That’s not something that can be taken for granted in a conservative, rural area.”

Alshebl’s first call after his victory was to his mother in Syria, who was thrilled with the news, SWR reported.

The Association of Municipalities of Baden-Württemberg said Alshebl is the first man with Syrian roots to run for and win a mayor’s office. He will start his role in June.

'You are not a refugee.' Roma refugees fleeing war in Ukraine say they are suffering discrimination and prejudice

Ostelsheim residents have welcomed their incoming mayor. “The fairy tale has come true, and the right man has become our mayor,” Annette Keck, who lives in the village, told SWR.

Strauss, one of his opponents, congratulated Alshebl. “I wish you good luck and at the same time ask for support for Mr. Alshebl, for our shared Ostelsheim,” he said on Facebook.

The state’s Integration Minister Manne Lucha said that Alshebl’s victory showed that diversity is a natural part of Baden-Württemberg. “I would be very pleased if Ryyan Alshebl’s election encourages more people with a migration history to run for political office,” he said.

Not everyone has been so warm to the 29-year-old. ZDF reported the Syrian received hateful comments on the campaign trail.

The young politician went from house to house, promoting his election program, and “the experiences were predominantly positive,” but there was also a minority of far-right fringe voters in Ostelsheim that did not want to accept him due to his Syrian roots, Alshebl told ZDF.

Born to a schoolteacher and agricultural engineer in Syria, Alshebl described his life as carefree until the age of 20, according to his campaign website.

At the time, protests against the Syrian government that began in 2011 soon devolved into chaotic war. The fighting and later rise of ISIS forced 10.6 million people from home by late 2015 – about half of Syria’s pre-war population.

Alshebl faced the dilemma of being drafted for military service with the Syrian army or leaving the country, according to his website.

While many Syrians were displaced internally or fled to countries in the region, others like Alshebl made the dangerous journey to Europe. He was 21 years old at the time, and said he crossed from Turkey to the Greek island of Lesbos in a rubber dinghy.

Former German Chancellor Angela Merkel had implemented a brief open-door policy in 2015 that saw the country take in about 1.2 million asylum seekers in the following years, including Alshebl.

They were welcomed into British homes. Celebrating their first Christmas together, Ukrainians wonder if that hospitality will last

The move sparked a backlash in Germany and the sudden growth of the far-right, anti-immigration Alternative for Germany (AfD) in the wake of summer 2015.

Once in Germany, Alshebl lived close to Ostelsheim and said at the time he felt “there is only one thing you can do: get back on your feet quickly and start investing in your own future quickly.”

For the last seven years he worked in the administration of Althengstett town hall, in a neighboring town. He drew from his experience, he said in his campaign, and made digital access to to public administration services one of priorities. Flexible childcare and climate protections are also on his agenda.

Alshebl, who is a member of the Green Party and now has German citizenship, pledged during his campaign that once elected as mayor he would move to Ostelsheim.

#241. More banks say customers using AI agents are on their own — even if they get hacked

Citibank, the third largest commercial bank in the United States, changed its terms of service this week, telling customers who use third-party AI tools for personal finance that anything that goes wrong is their problem — even if they get hacked.

AI agents, ranging from frontier lab models to purpose-built personal finance tools, can be given the credentials to your bank account and other online financial portals, with the idea that they can analyze spending and investment decisions, offer advice, and even handle transactions and bill payments.

But financial institutions aren’t thrilled with the unreliable AI actors in their systems, and are telling customers that they won’t be held responsible for reversing an intentional or unintentional transaction made by automated assistants.

“As third-party AI tools become more widely available, we want customers to understand the risks associated with sharing banking credentials or authorizing those tools to access their accounts,” a Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch. “We recently updated our Online User Agreement to provide greater clarity about those risks and to reinforce that customers who choose to grant account access to third-party AI agents are responsible for activities conducted through those agents.”

JPMorgan and Bank of America, the two largest banks in the country, announced similar policies last month. And PNC, the eighth largest bank, changed its terms today.

Citi’s update goes beyond telling customers that they are responsible for anything their agents may do; it also informs them that if the AI tool leaks their credentials or is hacked, the customer will be responsible for anything hackers do with their account. Banking giant Citizens similarly emailed its customers this week, warning that they are also on the hook for anything their AI assistant does, according to an email seen by TechCrunch.

As AI startups look for more revenue, breaking into personal finance presents an attractive opportunity for companies to add value for customers and wet their beaks in the stream of fees generated by the sector. But if consumers are already leery of sharing their email or chat records with an AI agent, bank and brokerage accounts offer a new trust challenge.

Anthropic’s approach is telling: The personal finance tool it launched earlier this month is explicitly billed as “read-only” and “can’t move, manage, or invest your money.”

Beyond cybersecurity concerns, banks also view efforts to enter their franchise as a competitive threat. Markets shuddered briefly in reaction to the prospect of AI tools making it easier for consumers to avoid unnecessary fees or low-interest deposit accounts that pad bank margins. But if AI agents become an important way to interact with financial decisions, banks will fight to control that pathway. Citi’s terms note that AI agents it provides can be used by customers without worrying about additional liability.

The reaction by banks is part of a broader trend among businesses of all stripes: AI labs promise that their agents will revolutionize the way people engage with a variety of service providers, then the service providers say “hold on a minute.” We’ve seen it in everything from car-hailing to food service, and how these relationships play out will help determine which AI investments pay off.

#240. Trump’s latest land grab is an AI hub in the Philippines

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Facing opposition at home, Trump is eyeing the former US colony to build chip fabs and data centers.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Facing opposition at home, Trump is eyeing the former US colony to build chip fabs and data centers.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

President Donald Trump was greeted by no less than five beauty queens when he landed in the Philippines on Tuesday, according to local news outlet Rappler. As he stepped onto the tarmac, a child handed him small US and Philippine flags. A band played “YMCA,” a staple at Trump rallies, as more girls in traditional bell-sleeved blouses and young men in wide-brimmed hats danced to the song.

Outside the airport, hundreds of protesters packed streets bearing signs — and at least one prop casket — saying “Trump out.” Others say “Reject Pax Silica,” the name for the US-led international initiative to secure chips and critical minerals used in the hardware fueling the AI boom.

“Pax” comes from the Latin word for “peace, stability, and long-term prosperity,” the Pax Silica website proudly says. Yet the unspoken question — peace and prosperity for whom? — explains the whiplash between the Philippine government’s warm welcome of Trump and the public fury raging outside.

Trump says he wants to “win AI” — whatever that means. As such, the US needs data centers full of GPUs to do it. With Americans increasingly opposed to data centers and chip factories being built in their communities, the US is now recruiting less affluent nations to take up some of the slack. Lawmakers from both the US and the Philippines are promising a slice of the riches that advances in generative AI are supposed to create. And that’s supposed to be worth the land razed, homes destroyed, and natural resources lost in the name of technological progress. In the Philippines, that’s taking the shape of a proposed 1,620-hectare (4,000 acres) hub for chip factories, processing plants for critical minerals, and data centers.

At least two dozen countries have signed on to the Pax Silica initiative, and Trump was expected to pressure more to join while in the Philippines for events related to the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit. With Pax Silica, the US aims to insulate itself from China’s dominance in manufacturing semiconductors and processing critical minerals needed to make those chips. Trump held a bilateral meeting with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. Wednesday before heading to Shenzhen to meet with China’s President Xi Jinping and Russia’s President Vladimir Putin.

Philippine and US officials are expected to sign a framework agreement by the end of the year for the jointly developed Pax Silica Economic Security Zone. The details are still murky and shrouded in NDAs. The Philippines’ Bases Conversion and Development Authority (BCDA) overseeing the plan expects an economic boom from minerals processing and chip manufacturing, with perhaps a data center or two on site. Meanwhile, human rights advocates who oppose the project warn that it will displace Indigenous residents, cutting them off from water resources on which they rely.

“How do we give up our burial grounds, our church, our livelihood?”

“We do not want to give up our community,” Petronila Capiz-Muñoz, chieftain of the Indigenous Aeta Hungey tribe, told Human Rights Watch. “How do we give up our burial grounds, our church, our livelihood? This is where we source our water, this is where we plant.” Capiz-Muñoz said BCDA officials have told residents that they’ll need to relocate by December to make way for development. Small farmers are also afraid the semiconductor hub will push them off their lands, according to Lian Buan, Southeast Asia researcher at Human Rights Watch.

There are layers of land-grabbing to unpack here.

The Aeta are among the first people to inhabit the Philippines, believed to have arrived around 30,000 years ago. And yet the Aeta Hungey tribe has been trying since 1994 to secure an official certificate recognizing its ancestral domain in one of the areas now facing industrial development. But since the tribe hasn’t been granted that certificate, the BCDA can still claim the AI hub will be built “outside titled ancestral domains.”

The agency says the project will be on public land located at the former Clark Air Base built in the early 1900s when the Philippines was a US territory. The Philippine constitution now only allows its own citizens to own private land. But the BCDA can offer leases lasting as long as 99 years to foreign developers. The agency expects to rake in close to $1 billion USD (about 60 billion in PHP Peso) from lease income over the first 25 years.

There are layers of land-grabbing to unpack here

The BCDA was in talks with around 30 different companies interested in setting up shop in Clark’s proposed AI hub, then-BCDA President and CEO Joshua Bingcang told reporters in July. He couldn’t name names, he said, because of NDAs — creating the same kind of opacity that has turned Americans against data centers on their home turf.

He did mention, however, that an American energy company was conducting a feasibility study into building a liquefied natural gas (LNG) line and plant to meet the hub’s enormous electricity demand. Bingcang called LNG a clean fuel, but that’s provably false. LNG is primarily methane, a smog-forming pollutant that is an even more potent greenhouse gas than carbon dioxide.

Bingcang said in September that the Philippine government wanted to invite Nvidia, TSMC, and Apple. While one or two data center developers had expressed interest, according to Bingcang, most of the companies interested were “high tech” manufacturing firms. He has also described chip manufacturing as a “clean” industry, echoing corporate spin that’s been around since the start of Silicon Valley.

At the first chip fabs in California, workers — many of them Filipino immigrants — donned “bunny suits” to assemble sensitive chips in environmentally controlled “clean rooms.” Little did they know that the gear they wore offered little protection against the harsh chemicals used in manufacturing, which also leaked out of storage tanks to contaminate soil and groundwater. When studies came out in the ’90s documenting disproportionately high rates of miscarriages among workers, companies pledged to phase out some of the chemicals linked to reproductive health risks. Around the same time, chip manufacturing started to move out of the US to Asia, where similar health problems stemming from the same chemicals cropped up even decades later.

Chipmakers say their fabs have gotten much safer over the years, in part because more work is automated. Even so, the BCDA estimates that the AI hub will create up to 190,000 direct jobs. The BCDA, White House, and Philippine’s Malacañang Palace did not immediately respond to questions from The Verge.

“I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great,” Trump told President Marcos during his visit. Marcos’ parents are estimated to have stolen up to $30 billion from government coffers over decades of rule before a people power movement ousted the current president’s father from the same office in the 1980s. But public memory, it seems, can be short.

And skyrocketing demand for more powerful chips used for generative AI has raised fears among environmental and labor groups that history might repeat itself. Companies move fast and break things, leaving a toxic mess in their wake. And when Americans decide they’ve had enough in their own back yards, the industry sets up shop somewhere else where they think their customers aren’t paying attention and politicians are happy to look the other direction.

#239. Google’s AI feature in Search gets its own incognito mode

Google’s search engine has long offered an incognito mode for all those search queries you don’t want logged or associated with your account. Now, Google’s AI Mode in Search is getting a similar feature. In a blog post, the company announced a new feature that brings “temporary threads” to AI Mode, where the conversations you have won’t be resurfaced later in areas like your search history or recommendations.

Many leading AI chatbots already offer the option for temporary chats, Google Gemini included. However, there was not yet an option to have a temporary chat when you’re searching Google and are dropped into its conversational interface, known as AI Mode.

Google says that users won’t have to adjust their settings to take advantage of the new option. Instead, they’ll be able to click a temporary threads icon in the upper right corner of AI Mode to engage in a temporary chat. This essentially works the same as a temporary chat would in the stand-alone Gemini chatbot.

The option could be convenient to use at those times when you start digging deeper into a particular topic via AI Mode and realize that you don’t want the discussion to become a part of your search history because of its personal nature.

Once the temporary thread is started, the session becomes detached from your account, Google says. This means that the responses the AI provides won’t be informed by past preferences, nor will they impact future personalization. That could also be useful for those times when you want to learn about something as more of a one-off, but don’t want Google to start to think of the topic as one of your main interests.

When you close out of the thread, you will return to searching as usual.

The company says the option to engage in temporary chats in AI Mode is rolling out globally on desktop and mobile across all supported languages.

#238. Absolute Batman can save DC Studios

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Skydance’s cinematic superhero universe could get back on track through DC’s progressive Absolute Universe comics.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With James Gunn and Peter Safran out as the co-heads of DC Studios following Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, it’s unclear what will become of the cinematic universe that was building around movies like last year’s Superman. Though Gunn’s Man of Tomorrowfeature might still come out, it seems like his larger vision for the DC Universe may be tossed out by DC Studios’ newly appointed CEO, Jeremy Latcham. Gunn was taking his time fleshing out the DCU with relatively less high-profile characters like Peacemaker, Supergirl, and Clayface. But Latcham has reportedly received a mandate from Skydance CEO David Ellison to fast-track a new version of the Justice League featuring more popular heroes like Wonder Woman and the Green Lantern.

This drastic shift in priorities will likely change what the DCU looks like in the coming years as Skydance attempts to parlay its comics IP into projects that can help it get on top of the $80 billion of debt the company is now responsible for after acquiring WBD. Ellison’s instinct to whip out the A-list superheroes is probably right from a business perspective. Most casual DC movie fans are more interested in stories about the Trinity than obscure characters like Gorilla Grodd. But after two iffy attempts at creating interconnected DC movie franchises, that audience might not have much of an appetite for yet another full-on reboot.

Rather than starting from scratch, DC Studios should probably keep some of the things that worked well during Gunn and Safran’s tenure, like presenting Superman as a compassionate person with a strong moral compass. But if the studio really wants to take this franchise in a new direction that gets people excited, it should also be thinking about tapping into the energy of some of DC’s most successful recent comics. Though the WBD merger prompted Skydance to begin laying off almost 2,000 employees last month, DC Comics — which Skydance gained control of as part of the deal — was reportedly left unscathed because the publishing arm’s business has been performing surprisingly well. Much of that success seems to have been driven by strong sales of comics created by DC’s Absolute Universe imprint.

Launched in 2024, the Absolute Universe comics reimagine DC’s heroes and villains as wildly different versions of their classic selves. Absolute Wonder Woman is a witch raised in Hell, Absolute Superman first arrives on Earth as a teenager who remembers his childhood on Krypton, and Absolute Batman is a working-class civil engineer rather than a socialite born into immense wealth. The Absolute characters’ worldviews and approaches to enacting justice are all distinct from their mainstream universe counterparts. But as much as the Absolute comics diverge from established canon, they each capture the essence of what makes their central characters iconic pillars of the DC brand.

Wonder Woman aside, there have been so many cinematic portrayals of DC characters based on their classic selves that it has often seemed like Warner Bros. was stuck in a kind of rut. There have been slight stylistic differences between the various big-screen iterations of Superman and Batman over the years, but for the most part, they have all come across as the products of a studio that’s averse to trying new things.

Absolute Batman is compelling because it inverts the established power structures that define most classic Batman comics.

That’s part of what made Matt Reeves’ The Batman— which didn’t bother to rehash Bruce Wayne’s tragic origin story — feel somewhat like a breath of fresh air when it debuted in 2022. Robert Pattinson will reprise his role as the Dark Knight in The Batman Part II, but it’s not really clear whether that version of the character will have any bearing on the DCU’s future.

Between Reeves’ Batman films existing in their own universe (similar to Todd Phillips’ Joker features) and Pattinson presumably being busy now that he’s been cast as Amazon’s new James Bond, it doesn’t seem likely that that version of the character will be incorporated into the DCU. Ellison obviously intends to put out more projects featuring Batman, though, and DC Studios could tell a riveting story if it took some cues from the hero’s Absolute incarnation. What makes Absolute Batman such a compelling series is the way it inverts the established power structures that define most classic Batman comics. In those books, Bruce Wayne is part of Gotham’s marginalized underclass, while the Joker is a moneyed elite whose villainy is highlighted in the way he manipulates various industries.

This inversion puts Absolute Batman in a position to operate as a vigilante with a much deeper connection to the ordinary people he has sworn to protect. Monstrous supervillains like Poison Ivy and Bane are still major threats, but the comics also spend time pitting Absolute Batman against more ordinary evils like corrupt police departments infested with white nationalists fixated on terrorizing minorities. Absolute Batman’s progressive political overtones might not gel with Ellison’s personal sensibilities, and the comic’s brutality probably doesn’t align with Latcham’s approach to making films. But Absolute Batman’s phenomenal sales have made it abundantly clear that readers are enjoying these stories, which is likely a big part why DC Studios announced earlier this year that it has plans to produce an animated series based on the comics.

Those plans might change in the wake of Skydance acquiring WBD, but an Absolute Batman series would give DC Studios an organic way to introduce the character into its tentpole films. Having characters jump back and forth between TV and the big screen was one of the stronger ideas Gunn introduced to the DCU, and it’s a strategy that could continue to benefit DC Studios under Latcham’s leadership. Turning Absolute Batman into the next flagship Bruce Wayne would be a bold move on Skydance’s part. But if the studio really wants to prove that this megamerger was the right decision, it needs to show the public that it has the guts to take exciting chances.

#237. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.

#235. Democrats skip a vote on adding Brendan Carr’s adviser to the FCC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Trump is stacking the FCC with Republicans, even as the Democrats boycott.

Trump is stacking the FCC with Republicans, even as the Democrats boycott.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Senate commerce committee Chair Ted Cruz (R-TX) began a meeting Wednesday meant to confirm President Donald Trump’s nominee to the Federal Communications Commission with a lopsided dais. To his right sat all 14 Republican members of the panel, including Senate Majority Leader John Thune (R-SD). To his left were about a dozen empty chairs — every Democratic member of the panel was absent.

Cruz said he’d learned the night before that Democrats intended to boycott the vote, though Democrats say they’d repeatedly raised their concerns about advancing a Republican FCC nominee without a Democratic one. “The administration has shown it’s willing to move Democrats,” Cruz said, pointing to Senate votes scheduled on bipartisan pairs of nominations to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission and the Securities and Exchange Commission. And even though the Supreme Court confirmed his power to do so, Cruz said, Trump has not moved to fire any Democrats from the FCC, as he did at the Federal Trade Commission. “This is an ordinary nomination to fill a vacancy previously held by someone in the same party,” Cruz said.

“This is an ordinary nomination to fill a vacancy previously held by someone in the same party”

But in statements and comments to reporters outside the committee room, Democrats said that the process has been far from normal. Senate committees often vote on nominations to historically independent agencies in bipartisan pairs. Confirming Danielle Thumann Severs, senior counsel to FCC Chair Brendan Carr, to become a commissioner at the agency would put the FCC at four total members — three Republicans and one Democrat, Anna Gomez. Gomez’s term has technically expired, though she can remain on the commission through next year unless she’s replaced. But Democrats fear that by confirming a third Republican to the FCC, Trump might finally fire Gomez, because he’d no longer need her present for a quorum to vote on key actions.

Democrats asked for Thumann’s confirmation vote to be withheld until her nomination could be paired with a Democratic nomination. One way to do that would be to renominate Gomez. But so far, the president has not offered any Democratic nominees for the commission. Democrats said they made their concerns clear to Cruz, and added, “This is not a problem we created, but a problem that the White House can solve.” The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In addition to the procedural concerns, Democrats worry about Thumann’s work as an adviser to Carr, and whether she was truthful about the matters she worked on. In a letter asking Cruz to delay her confirmation vote, Sen. Ben Ray Luján (D-NM) detailed his concerns. At the November 10th hearing where Thumann answered committee members’ questions, she testified that she had “not worked with the Chairman on any media-related issues,” Luján noted in the letter. But documents obtained via a Freedom of Information Act request show that Thumann was “looped in” on various media matters, and “helped draft Skydance’s commitments that facilitated the 2 FCC’s approval of the Skydance-Paramount merger,” Luján wrote. “She also played a role in processing informal complaints related to CBS 60 Minutes alleged news distortion and she apparently orchestrated the Commission’s response to consumers regarding the Jimmy Kimmel controversy.” The FCC did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the matter.

Even Cruz admitted concerns with Carr’s handling of Kimmel’s comments in the aftermath of conservative activist Charlie Kirk’s killing. But at Wednesday’s meeting, he said he “emphatically agree[s]” that Thumann had resolved any lingering worries about that. He added that his JAWBONE Act, which the committee previously passed, could directly address First Amendment violations by government agencies of either party. The bill would let individuals sue government officials if they illegally try to compel social media or broadcast companies to remove their speech. Sen. Jerry Moran (R-KS), who’d similarly expressed concern over the Kimmel incident, said he found Thumann’s responses on the subject “comforting,” including in a phone call earlier that morning where he said he’d expressed his “serious interest in how the FCC protects and promotes free speech, including free speech on broadcast.” On the call, he said, “she was able to satisfy me once again in her answers, including an explanation for her role at the FCC and her lack of engagement on this type of issue as the FCC made decisions.”

“If she was lying about this, what else is she lying about and what else is she hiding?”

Speaking to reporters outside of the hearing room ahead of the meeting, Luján accused Thumann of lying to the committee. “The questions that I have is if she was lying about this, what else is she lying about and what else is she hiding?” he said. Luján said he wants both Thumann and Carr to appear before the committee by the end of the year to answer lawmakers’ questions. Asked if he’d support using the committee’s subpoena power or even pursuing impeachment against Carr when Democrats regain power next year, Luján said his vote to confirm Carr as a commissioner under President Joe Biden is among his biggest regrets, and that while he hopes Carr will testify voluntarily, “everything should be on the table here to be able to get answers for the American people.”

When it came time to vote on whether to advance Thumann’s nomination for a final step of a vote on the Senate floor, all 15 Republicans voted “aye.” That gave Thumann enough votes for her nomination to advance to the Senate floor, where it’s expected to pass along party lines once scheduled. Still, the clerk listed off the names of the 13 Democratic committee members whose seats remained empty. There was no response.

#234. What makes 'Grand Theft Auto VI' more than just a game?

When "GTA VI" is released on November 19, millions of people worldwide will rush to their consoles to play it. How has this video game created such a cross-generational buzz?

"Oh, it's so on now!" is the tagline in the latest trailer for the video game "Grand Theft Auto VI," or "GTA VI" for short.

"GTA VI" is a game of superlatives that's been crashing through records. The hype around it is unparalleled. No game in history has clocked so many pre-orders. It's thought that around 10 million people have already purchased it. There's no denying it: "GTA VI" is a global cultural phenomenon.

A few days before its release on November 19, fans can already start downloading the game. The file is so large that downloading it can take several days, depending on the internet connection. Providers have already registered a significant spike in data traffic.

As soon as the game is unlocked, players can start speeding through the fictional US metropolis of Vice City in a stylish sports car with criminal couple Jason and Lucia.

To mark the launch of "Grand Theft Auto VI," its real-life inspiration — Miami — is getting a full-scale "Vice City" makeover. This promotional push includes the Miami Heat basketball team stepping onto the hardwood in custom "Vice City" jerseys for a home game, a local festival featuring artists from the game's official soundtrack, and roadside "Vice City" signage installed across the area.

Expectations for "GTA VI" are sky-high. Nearly 5,000 people have been working on the game for more than 10 years. Development costs are estimated at around $1 billion (€900 million).

"Based on those figures alone — as well as the series' extremely successful and popular track record — this can certainly be described as a truly exceptional release," says Rudolf Thomas Inderst, cultural studies scholar and professor of Game Design and Game Studies at Germany's Neu-Ulm University of Applied Sciences.

When such an effort is put into a product, it's only natural for people to expect great things, he adds. Inderst is expecting "GTA VI" to showcase the absolute best of what modern gaming can do across gameplay, storytelling, visuals and tech.

This video game series has been around for almost three decades, setting new standards through its different versions. A biting satire on US society and the American dream, "GTA" parodies politics, culture and the media.

The game is also renowned for its gameplay freedom and spectacularly staged chaos in a crazy world where players can do whatever they please. Shoot down helicopters with a bazooka, go to the hairdresser's, take a stroll on the beach, go to a nightclub, take part in car races and much more.

What makes it a standout among video games is how its NPCs — non-player characters, those not being controlled by a human player — are brought to life. They respond based on a player's interactions with them, displaying individualized behaviors and traits ranging from fear to hostility. "GTA VI" has an estimated 600,000 animations for the civilians in the fictional state of Leonida.

"GTA VI" is essentially a huge adult playground, striking a chord across several generations. Millennials grew up with "Grand Theft Auto III," "Grand Theft Auto: Vice City," "Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas," and "Grand Theft Auto IV," while Gen Z grew up with "Grand Theft Auto V." Generation Alpha might still be too young for the latest edition, but will likely find ways to play it anyway.

Social media has certainly played a part in the series' ongoing success, particularly "GTA V" and its multiplayer spin-off, "GTA Online." Numerous content creators stream gameplay sessions on Twitch or YouTube, and particularly funny or crazy gameplay moments end up on TikTok.

At the same time, a dedicated role-play community has grown around the game. Players gather inside the world of "GTA" to act out specific characters, such as taxi drivers, judges or small-time criminals. They invent realistic backstories for their personas, and many even use accents to stay in character.

The hype surrounding "GTA VI" is not only good news for the game itself but could also boost the entire games industry by drawing more people back to their consoles. This is because the boom triggered by the COVID-19 pandemic was followed by studio closures and redundancies. Worldwide, it's estimated that 50,000 people in the games industry have lost their jobs since 2022.

But while publisher Rockstar Games is well-known for the meticulous polish of its titles and attention to detail, the perfectionism so prized by fans also has its drawbacks, especially for those working on the game. Rockstar, like many other game developers, has frequently faced major criticism regarding its labor practices.

Former developers report extremely long working hours and a heavy workload that resulted in health issues. Trade unions accuse company managers of employee surveillance, resulting in a culture of workplace stress and low morale.

Coinciding with the launch of Rockstar's largest game to date, the developers behind it are pushing for trade union recognition. That move would give them a collective voice to address working hours, pay, pensions and parental leave. If successful, their campaign could set a major precedent for the wider video game industry, which continues to face challenges around fair labor practices.

This article was originally written in German.

#233. Spend management platform Payhawk announces $280M raise, 2B valuation

U.K.-based fintech Payhawk announced a $280 million Series C funding round Wednesday, led by Goldman Sachs’ growth equity arm, making it one of Europe’s largest Series C rounds this year, as noted by Sifted. The round values the company at $2 billion, bringing its total funding to more than $500 million to date.

Founded in 2018, Payhawk is an enterprise financial management platform that helps companies track and manage expenses and procurement using AI agents. Speaking to FinTech magazine, Payhawk’s co-founder and CEO, Hristo Borisov, said the company saw “an incredible growth opportunity and room to bring finance teams AI-native products that combine agents, payment infrastructure, and enterprise controls.”

Payhawk competes with spend management companies Pleo, Moss, and Spendesk, and says it operates in 33 countries. Notably, the company said it still has 70% of the $100 million it raised in 2022, a round that valued the company at $1 billion. This fresh capital will support its global expansion. Other investors in the round include Greenoaks, Lightspeed, and QED.

#232. The nation’s largest school districts consider ditching Google Search

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is trying to force Gemini on schools, and they’re not thrilled.

Google is trying to force Gemini on schools, and they’re not thrilled.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

New York, Los Angeles, and Chicago schools are considering Google alternatives after the company said it will disable Search for schools that opt out of its AI features, according to The New York Times. Starting next year, schools will not have the option to disable Gemini-powered features without also disabling Search. Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president of education, told the Times that, “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” and that schools that turn off Google’s AI features “will not have access to Search.”

Many elected officials, including New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani and Senator Bernie Sanders, responded harshly, calling Google’s behavior “extortion.” Mamdani had recently announced a policy that barred 2-K-8 students from using AI in the classroom. In a statement to the Times, New York City schools chancellor Kamar H. Samuels said, “If Google doesn’t reconsider their position, we will explore other search options.” Chicago’s public schools have also reportedly told Google that if it can’t provide a version of Search without AI, “the district will look to other search solutions.”

In a statement to The Verge, Google spokesperson Laura Breen said, “We hear parents and teachers’ concerns about students using AI to shortcut learning, and we share the same goal: help students learn. To improve Search for the classroom, we’re starting to pilot Guided Learning capabilities in Search this month to walk students step-by-step through a range of complex problems, instead of just giving quick answers. Next year, we’re also updating our approach to controls for schools to address feedback we’ve heard from educators, so Gemini app, Gemini Notebook and Search will be off by default until schools decide which services to enable. We are actively building these experiences in partnership with educators, listening closely to ensure our products support students and teachers.”

Educators are wary of AI’s effect on students’ critical thinking skills, and some studies have shown that students who use AI generally perform worse on tests. There are also concerns about the mental health effects on children who develop relationships with chatbots at a young age. But Google is deeply embedded in classrooms across the US, which rely on Google Classroom, Chromebooks, and other services, and going fully Google-free might prove difficult.

#230. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.
