#229. Snapchat now lets developers build AR Lenses with AI assistants like Claude Code and Cursor

Snapchat announced on Wednesday that it’s giving Lens creators the ability to build Lenses using the AI coding assistants they already use, like Claude Code, Cursor, and Codex. Snapchat Lenses, which are a core part of the social network’s platform, are AR effects that let users alter their appearance, add digital objects to their surroundings, or interact with animated experiences through the camera.

The company is introducing Easy Lens CLI, which builds on its existing Easy Lens tool, which lets creators generate Lenses from a single prompt on the web and mobile. Easy Lens CLI brings that same idea into developers’ existing workflows, allowing AI assistants to handle the technical work while creators focus on the vision for the Lens itself.

The launch indicates that Snapchat is looking to lower the barrier to creating AR content, while making its platform more accessible to developers who are increasingly using AI coding assistants. Instead of requiring creators to learn how to use Snapchat’s own development tools, the company is bringing Lens creation into the AI coding workflows they already use, which could in turn expand the pool of people who build for Snapchat.

Developers enrolled in Snapchat’s Lens+ Payouts program can earn from the Lenses they create with Easy Lens CLI. Snapchat says it paid out $1.8 million to Lens+ community creators last month.

Lens creators can describe the Lens they want in plain language, and their AI assistant handles the process from start to finish, Snapchat says. That includes building with more than 100 AR building blocks and 50 AI asset generators, generating previews, and publishing them to Snapchat and tracking their performance.

With Easy Lens CLI, Snapchat says developers can create everything from Web Lens games with leaderboards to camera effects and beauty filters.

AI assistants can generate assets, build and test Lenses, address errors, and send updated versions directly to the creator’s phone. The new capability can take a game from an initial idea to a published Lens in a single day, without developers having to write code themselves, Snapchat says.

The tool is aimed at different types of creators. The company notes that game developers can bring existing browser games to Snapchat as Web Lenses, while AI creators can use assets from their preferred tools to build Lenses in their own style. Additionally, agencies and brands can turn existing campaign videos, images, and other brand assets into AR experiences with less production time.

Easy Lens CLI runs on Mac, Windows, and Linux.

#228. Elon Musk tries to explain what’s going on with Ukraine and Starlink

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The SpaceX CEO claims that Ukraine president Volodymyr Zelenskyy is ‘lying’ about using Starshield.

The SpaceX CEO claims that Ukraine president Volodymyr Zelenskyy is ‘lying’ about using Starshield.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Elon Musk said Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy is lying about disruptions to Starshield in Ukraine and that he’s trying to misuse SpaceX’s consumer service, Starlink, for military purposes. Musk made the comments at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday as he tried to explain the conflict following weeks of back and forth between Zelenskyy, Musk, and the US government.

“From the beginning, I’ve said that Starlink is a civilian system,” Musk said. “It is not meant to be used for offensive drone strikes that kill people. If you want to do that, you need to do Starshield, which is a separate system that is controlled by the US government. But do not ask me as a private individual to make kill decisions.”

Musk also said that “if they want to do something that’s an offensive military action, it needs to be the Starshield system where the kill decision is that of the War Department, perhaps ultimately the president, and not mine.”

The interviewer, Andrew Ross Sorkin, said that President Zelenskyy refers to the system as Starshield and is claiming that is the system that was slowed down. “That’s false,” Musk said, claiming that Zelenskyy is “not always truthful.”

Sorkin asked, “So when President Zelenskyy talks about using Starshield, he is not using Starshield, or he’s lying?”

“He is lying,” Musk said. “What he is actually saying is he wants to use Starlink for offensive military operations.”

#226. Sam Altman says his life is so weird that he’s unfazed by Andrew Garfield portraying him in upcoming movie

Hollywood has found plenty of material and cinematic inspiration from a string of Silicon Valley scandals and power struggles in recent years.

“You Can See Everything,” the documentary about Elizabeth Holmes, smashed box office expectations, and Netflix’s limited series “The Altruists” comes out Thursday to tell the story of Sam Bankman-Fried and the downfall of FTX.

As the founder and CEO of OpenAI, one of the most influential companies of the century, Sam Altman isn’t going to escape the Hollywood treatment, either.

In “Artificial,” a biographical comedy-drama opening in theaters on Christmas, the lauded actor Andrew Garfield will portray Altman in a film that chronicles OpenAI’s rise and the internal turmoil that erupted when he was briefly ousted in 2023.

“If you had told me 20 years ago that someone was going to make a movie about me, I think that would have struck me as … that would have been a very weird thing,” Altman said onstage at the New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday. “Now it doesn’t feel like it’s in like the top 10 weirdest things about my life.”

Garfield is best known for his role as Spider-Man, but his big break came in 2010, when he played Facebook co-founder Eduardo Saverin in “The Social Network” alongside Jesse Eisenberg as Mark Zuckerberg. (That film also had a sequel of sorts this year called “The Social Reckoning,” but it bombed at the box office.)

So, if Altman isn’t fazed by the looming release of “Artificial,” what’s actually the weirdest thing in his life?

“The fact that we are ushering in this technology that we have all of these very real concerns about, and that we have, we find ourselves in a role of such significance and such responsibility to make that go well,” Altman said. “That’s a crazy situation to find yourself in.”

Sure, that is pretty weird, Sam. It’s also weird that you use ChatGPT for parenting advice and don’t respect olive oil. But I’d wager to say it’s even weirder that there’s an upcoming film about the time your colleagues briefly ousted you because they were concerned that you can’t be trusted.

Say what you want about Mark Zuckerberg (I certainly have), but at least he’s honest about what he thinks about “The Social Network.”

“It was weird, man,” Zuckerberg reflected on a podcast last year. “They got all these very specific details of what I was wearing, or these specific things correct, but then the whole narrative arc around my motivations and all this stuff were like, completely wrong … The whole arc is like, I’m somehow motivated by trying to find a girlfriend, [but] I was dating Priscilla [Chan] before I started Facebook.”

Zuckerberg said he knew his employees would be tempted to see the movie, so he organized a company outing when it was released, even though the film hardly paints a flattering portrait of him.

Zuckerberg even had enough of a sense of humor to buy a prop from the film at auction. He owns an Ardsley High School T-shirt that Eisenberg wore in the movie and even wore it himself in a video with YouTubers Colin and Samir.

Eisenberg hasn’t been particularly complimentary of Zuckerberg, saying that he doesn’t want to think of himself as “associated with somebody like that.” And while “Artificial” hasn’t hit theaters yet, Garfield doesn’t seem to have a much more favorable opinion of the tech founder he portrays.

“I don’t know Sam Altman. I’ve never met him,” Garfield told Esquire. “But I think anyone who takes on that level of responsibility for a company or technology that you know is going to do a hell of a lot of damage to the soul of humanity [must have] a certain ability to dissociate.”

#224. Palmer Luckey thinks China is ‘thrilled to have Jensen out,’ so he wants the ousted Nvidia CEO reinstated

Anduril CEO Palmer Luckey thinks Nvidia’s board is giving China exactly what it wants by removing the company’s longtime CEO and co-founder, Jensen Huang.

Nvidia, the leading semiconductor designer whose chips power much of the AI industry, is undergoing a critical, unexpected leadership change. Huang, who led the company for 33 years, stepped down last week amid several controversies, including his extramarital affair with an alleged Chinese spy who has been investigated for possibly smuggling Nvidia chips into China to circumvent U.S. export restrictions.

“China wants Jensen out, so I want him in,” Luckey said onstage at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, repeating a statement he posted on X last week. “They’re thrilled to have Jensen out… There’s a reason it’s knocking a trillion dollars off Nvidia’s market cap. They know it makes America weaker to knock him out.”

Luckey disagrees with Huang’s longstanding position that Nvidia should have more leeway to export chips to China. But he also recognizes that China stands to benefit if its domestic chipmakers can challenge Nvidia’s dominance.

“China doesn’t want Nvidia to sell GPUs to Chinese companies,” Luckey said. “There are Chinese companies that want to buy Nvidia GPUs… China will not give them an import permit because they want to build an internal domestic processor industry… not build one that is dependent on the United States.”

Nvidia has said as much itself — the U.S. government granted the company license to ship some of its powerful H200 chips to certain Chinese customers, but those sales were restricted by the Chinese government. Nvidia’s interim CEO Colette Kress said during the company’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday that it will no longer sell H200 chips in China at all.

Luckey is far from alone among industry peers in his defense of Huang.

More than 200,000 people — including Elon Musk and the CEOs of Y Combinator, Databricks, Epic Games, Shopify, and Replit — have signed a petition calling for Nvidia to reinstate its founder. But Nvidia executives said during the company’s earnings call on Tuesday that the company does not have plans to reappoint Huang, even though the founder offered to return.

#220. Palmer Luckey warns AI-powered cyberattacks are outpacing defenses: ‘It’s time to start running’

AI-powered cyberattacks are moving faster than outdated technology systems can defend against them, Palmer Luckey warned at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday. Addressing the recent spate of tech outages, the Oculus founder and Anduril Industries co-founder argued that older systems are struggling to keep pace with the new hacking capabilities enabled by AI and warned it was time to quicken the pace of securing our defenses.

“We’re seeing the natural effects of a lot of these advanced AI tools allowing for the creation of various exploits,” he said.

While government officials largely attributed the latest set of technical issues to companies rushing to patch their systems to protect against the new vulnerabilities AI has discovered, Luckey focused on the underlying security challenges facing organizations using outdated technology.

Luckey explained that U.S. adversaries are currently exploiting the mismatch between new attack capabilities and our defense capabilities. These attacks are being carried out for both strategic and financial reasons, and North Korea was particularly involved, he added.

The interviewer, Andrew Ross Sorkin, noted that U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance had canceled his appearance at the Times’ conference this week because of the recent tech outages, saying he now had to attend meetings about the matter.

Given the situation, Sorkin asked Luckey how worried we should be about all this.

“It depends on how well prepared you are,” was Luckey’s answer. (He then ominously warned, “You guys are going to find out what’s going on soon enough.”)

The tech executive argued that AI could ultimately give defenders the upper hand, but they’d have to move more quickly.

“In the long run, the advantages are going to accrue to the side that is most effectively using AI for defense,” Luckey said. “With the people who say that everything can be hacked — they are right, for now. That actually may be the case with certain new techniques that are coming along. But as of right now, there’s a mismatch between the attackers who are very fast-moving and using technology and old, outdated systems that are not able to adopt technology as quickly as it is developed,” Luckey continued.

“And I think … people are going to have to pick up the pace,” he said. “It’s time to start running.”

#219. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#218. Muslims to outnumber Christians by 2035, research shows

Pew Research Center projections show that there will be 2.57 billion Muslims and 2.52 billion Christians by 2035. Researchers say differences in fertility rates account for the change.

Muslims are going to outnumber Christians by 2035, according to projections from the Washington-based Pew Research Center.

Pew said that while both religions are growing, Islam is growing faster than Christianity.

The main reason is Muslims tend to have more children than other major religious groups, according to the study.

"It's the babies,” Pew senior demographer Conrad Hackett told the AP news agency.

Pew data shows that Muslim women had an average of 3.1 children between 2020 and 2025, compared with 2.5 for Christian women. For all other women, the average was 1.6, lower than the 2.1 needed to replace each generation.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to the center's estimates, there were 2.27 billion Christians and 2.02 billion Muslims worldwide in 2020.

New projections show that there will be 2.57 billion Muslims and 2.52 billion Christians by 2035. If current trend persists, the global Muslim population is projected to exceed 3.6 billion by 2070, while the number of Christians is expected to fall just short of 3 billion.

According to the report, if recent trends continue, by 2070:

Alan Cooperman, Pew's director of religion research, told AP news agency that most of the projected growth of Muslims and Christians was occurring in countries where they already are the majority. He says conversion plays only a minor role.

"I don't think this is a situation in which the growth of Muslims is coming at the expense of Christians. I do worry about mischief being made with headlines or narratives that take this finding and portray it as sort of a clash where Christians are losing to Muslims,” he said.

Gina Zurlo, a senior researcher at Harvard Divinity School specializing in religious demographics, said inter-religious relations are "extremely important" in light of the trends.

"I hope people seek opportunities for greater inter-religious education. Personal contact is important — when people have personal relationships across religious differences, it ‘humanizes' the ‘other' in fruitful ways," she said.

#216. Nvidia created a weird bit of intrigue by not filing a routine SEC report

During Nvidia’s quarterly call on Tuesday, CFO-turned-interim-CEO Colette Kress dropped this little oddity: She said Nvidia wouldn’t be immediately filing its quarterly results report, known as a 10-Q, with the SEC. “We plan to file our 10-Q in the coming weeks.” And she offered no reason why.

The delay was widely noticed. Among the many comments on X about it, economic and investment analyst Edward Dowd called it a “red flag.” Nvidia has typically filed the 10-Q the same day it reports results.

The 10-Q offers far more detail about a company’s operations than an earnings press release, including sections that disclose “legal proceedings,” which include lawsuits that could affect the company; and “risk factors,” which cover other potentially business-impairing items.

The announcement of the delay was so unusual that UBS analyst Timothy Arcuri asked about it after the public earnings call. Nvidia told him the delay was related to whether the company would need to disclose additional information about its ongoing investigation into Huang in the 10-Q.

“The company announced that the 10-Q will be filed ‘in the coming weeks’ – later than usual. When queried on our callback, management indicated this was related to the ongoing investigation and whether any additional risk factors would need to be added to the filing,” Arcuri wrote in his research note. (Arcuri still rates the stock a “buy” by the way, although he did lower his price target to $220 from $240. The stock is now trading around $150).

Notably, on the same day that Nvidia reported earnings, The Wall Street Journal reported that the SEC is investigating Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors.

So the mystery thickens: What will Nvidia disclose? We’ll have to wait and see. (Nvidia has not yet responded to our request for comment.)

The delay suggests the company may have more to say about its mounting legal troubles. Nvidia is now facing multiple federal investigations over allegations that its partners were selling restricted Nvidia AI chips to China. So far, the scrutiny has come from a number of congressional committees; the Department of Justice looking into Megaspeed, the company run by a woman with whom Huang had an affair and who is also suspected of being a Chinese spy; and now, reportedly, the SEC.

Additionally, Nvidia is conducting its own internal investigation into Huang after he admitted to having an affair with Alice Huang (the Megaspeed executive), and allegations surfaced that he also had affairs with other Nvidia employees.

There’s little doubt that Wall Street will scrutinize that 10-Q once it’s finally filed. The intrigue brings to mind the old saying: “Doctors bury their mistakes. Lawyers put theirs in prison. And accountants hide theirs in the footnotes.”

#214. Palmer Luckey doesn’t deny that he’s a prepper

Defense mogul Palmer Luckey — whose $90 billion drone and weapons startup Anduril is flush with government contracts — is still the kind of guy who prepares for the worst.

During Luckey’s conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the billionaire implied that Americans should batten down the hatches in case there are rough times ahead. Luckey himself has taken steps to prepare for a potential cataclysmic event, he said.

The conversation took place against the backdrop of a series of recent large-scale outages — at Apple, at Microsoft, at the NSA, and at the Federal Aviation Administration — that have led to concerns that America may be under attack by cyberthreat actors. The government has said that this is not the case, but Luckey cryptically teased that there may be more to the story.

“Look, all the 4chan schizo posters are like 12 hours ahead of the mainstream media on this. You guys are going to find out what’s going on soon enough,” he said.

Sorkin also asked Luckey to expound on comments made backstage “about what might be a cyber war that seems to be going on around us.”

Luckey described a “mismatch” between the sophisticated AI-powered cyberattacks that exist today and “old, outdated systems that are not able to adopt technology as quickly as it is developed.” He added: “I think people are going to have to pick up the pace. It’s time to start running.”

“What does that mean? Are you a prepper?” Sorkin asked.

Luckey didn’t deny the characterization. “I own a missile base 200 feet underground,” he answered, to audience laughter. “It’s a funny joke, but a missile base is actually a pretty bad way to run from the apocalypse. Everyone knows exactly where you are, and they [bases] are designed to take a hit from a nuclear weapon, a blast overpressure event.”

However, he had some qualifications on the prepper label. “The point is, like everyone should have three days of water. Everyone should have three days of dry food. If you have prescription medication, don’t go refill it when you run out. Make sure you refill it 72 hours before you run out.”

“People used to be taught this in middle school, and now everyone who remembers that is treated like a nut,” he added.

It should be pointed out that preparing for the apocalypse is not necessarily a new interest for Luckey. In 2017, a person familiar with the billionaire described him as a “prepper” and the bunker that was broached Wednesday is one of several decommissioned Cold War era underground bases that Luckey has purchased over the years. In 2024, he revealed that his massive video game collection is housed in one of them.

#212. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#210. Israel launches new ground operation in Gaza

Israel has said its military is targeting Hamas infrastructure in central Gaza and urged civilians to avoid the area. The fight is unfolding against the backdrop of a dire food and shelter situation for Palestinians.

Israel says its military is involved in operations on Wednesday in central Gaza in an effort to dislodge Hamas militants.

France's AFP news agency reported "intense Israeli artillery shelling amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes."

A statement from the Israeli military (IDF) said, "The IDF launched a precise operation within Gaza earlier today west of the Yellow Line to remove a threat posed to IDF troops operating in the Gaza Strip."

The Yellow Line in Gaza was established in an October 2025 ceasefire between Israel and Hamas, and separates Israeli and Hamas-controlled territory.

On Wednesday, COGAT, an Israeli military body responsible for civilian matters in the Palestinian territories warned international aid agencies and Palestinian civilians to avoid areas near the Al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps and Gaza's main thoroughfare, the Salah al-Din road.

Israel's military countered that it was reacting to Hamas ceasefire violations and the group's attempts to rearm.

Beyond the threat of violent gunfire and airstrikes, civilians in Gaza continue to face ongoing food insecurity and lack of adequate shelter as winter approaches.

On Tuesday, the International Organization for Migration (IOM) and the UN's World Food Programme (WFP) warned that funding shortfalls and access restrictions put untold numbers of Palestinian lives at risk.

Currently, more than 1.7 million Gazans are displaced, with IOM and WFP saying 1.4 million face acute hunger and 1.98 million require shelter assistance.

With Gaza's infrastructure decimated by years of Hamas mismanagement and pounding Israeli attacks, more than 80% of Palestinians are unemployed, meaning they cannot afford food and have been forced to burn garbage to try to keep warm.

"Families in Gaza are facing their first sleepless nights of a very long winter ahead," said WFP Palestine Country Director Shaun Hughes. "Unless we get immediate support to scale up the humanitarian response, we risk this becoming not only a shelter crisis but also a deeper food security crisis."

#209. Anthropic prices IPO at $83 a share, above projected range

Anthropic has priced its long-awaited IPO at $83 per share, above its initial range of $70 to $78, signaling strong investor appetite for one of the companies at the center of the AI boom, despite market jitters following the departure of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, which shook confidence in the industry. The Claude maker sold 980 million shares in the offering, up from the 825 million initially planned, generating $81.3 billion in proceeds and surpassing SpaceX’s $75 billion record to become the largest IPO in history by proceeds raised.

At the IPO price, Anthropic is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion on a fully diluted basis, a significant increase from the $965 billion the company reached in May when it raised $65 billion in its last private market round, but below the $2 trillion it was reportedly aiming for several months ago.

Anthropic will begin its life as a public company on Thursday, trading on Nasdaq under the ticker symbol “ANT.” A strong first day of trading would signal that investors remain optimistic about AI adoption, despite recent volatility in AI-driven stocks.

The company’s performance is expected to set expectations for private AI companies, including Anthropic’s main rival OpenAI, which was last reportedly valued at $1.25 billion but has yet to announce plans for an IPO.

Although none of Anthropic’s venture capital investors holds more than a 5% stake in the company, the IPO is a massive windfall for its numerous backers. Investors who own approximately 1% of the company reportedly include Sequoia, as well as former Google CEO Eric Schmidt. Singaporean sovereign wealth fund GIC also holds about a 1% stake in the company, according to a person familiar with the matter. Other backers set to make billions are Iconiq, Menlo Ventures, Coatue, and Sound Ventures, a firm launched by Guy Oseary and actor Ashton Kutcher. Spark Capital, the only VC firm known to hold a board seat at the company (it was the first institutional investor in the company, leading its $450 million Series C round in 2023 that valued the company at $4.5 billion), also stands to make a fortune off its early investment in Anthropic.

Some of the Anthropic’s existing investors, namely Sequoia, Iconiq, Lightspeed, Accel, and Altimeter Capital Management, are so enthusiastic about Anthropic’s future that they are reportedly planning to buy billions of dollars’ worth of the company’s shares in the IPO.

Yet the investors’ holdings in Anthropic pale in comparison to those of the company’s strategic partners Amazon, which owns nearly 15% of the company, and Google, which holds almost 14%. A group of major tech companies, including AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC Partners, have also agreed to purchase $26 billion worth of non-voting shares through a private placement alongside the IPO.

The company’s seven co-founders, including CEO Dario Amodei, each sold nearly 3.4 million shares in the IPO, according to the S-1 filing, bringing in about $282 million for each.

This post has been updated with the number of shares sold and IPO proceeds.

#207. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#204. Satya Nadella predicts AI agents will reshuffle the economics of the web

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella predicts that the rise of AI agents is going to reshape the economics of the internet, impacting the business models of the companies that rely on advertising and what he referred to as “human web” traffic.

Speaking at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the tech executive argued that the shift to an agentic web could change which companies pay others for traffic and for transactions, similar to how the transition from web portals like Yahoo to search engines like Google did years ago.

“The current aggregators who have a business model which was for the human web will get, in some sense, reshuffled,” said Nadella. “And if you were monetizing ads because of the eyeballs, you now have to probably get down to essentially maybe CPC [cost-per-click] wins; maybe it’s more transactional,” he continued. “And so if you don’t accept the agent, somebody else will.”

The discussion turned to Amazon and its decision to block AI agents from accessing its retail website, preventing them from browsing its catalog and making purchases on behalf of their users. Nadella suggested that companies trying to protect their existing business models could lose out to others that are more willing to embrace the AI agents. For instance, if Amazon continues blocking agents, another shopping platform such as Shopify, could allow them, and gain an advantage.

“I think this is going to be interesting,” he said, speaking to the changing models. “It’s the same thing that’s happening in other categories, right? Which is, if you’re in love with a particular business model, you have to kind of scramble,” he said.

#202. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#200. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella remains confident in Nvidia after Huang’s exit

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella weighed in on former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s unexpected departure amid a series of shocking controversies.

“It’s all sort of pretty tragic in the sense of, you know, Jensen is somebody I’ve known for a long time. I’ve admired what he’s done, and at this point, I kind of want to give him space to figure this out,” Nadella said onstage at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday.

Huang stepped down last week from his CEO role, which he has held for 33 years. Huang’s leadership has been central to Nvidia’s ascent to one of the world’s most valuable companies, but his judgment was questioned after it was reported that he had an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. officials believe is a Chinese spy feeding information back to Beijing. It was also revealed that Huang was having affairs with two Nvidia employees, including one who was a direct report.

While the world waits to see what the long-term economic impacts of Huang’s departure might be, Nadella says he still has confidence in Nvidia, which is a close partner of Microsoft.

“I’ll just say this one thing,” Nadella added. “Colette [Kress, interim CEO at Nvidia] worked at Microsoft for a long time. I’ve known her for a long time. She’s a tremendous person… I’m in touch with the same set of people I was in touch with before [Huang left], so I have a lot of confidence in the Nvidia team and their ability to continue to do the good work.”

Microsoft is not unlike Nvidia in that its founder Bill Gates inhabited a larger-than-life persona as CEO.

“Founders are unbelievable in the sense that they create something from nothing, so that is pretty magical… They’re singular, but they’re not gods, right?” Nadella said. “I used to say to Bill [Gates]… Founders also die, and so at some point, a company, if it needs to succeed, it has to have someone take over.”

#197. Elon Musk says ousting Jensen Huang is like ‘kicking Michael Jordan off your team’

Elon Musk has already been vocal about his disapproval of the Nvidia board’s decision to oust its founder and CEO Jensen Huang after a series of extramarital affairs were made public, including one with an alleged Chinese spy who is suspected of feeding sensitive information back to Beijing. At The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, Musk elaborated on his thoughts about Nvidia’s leadership change.

“Jensen is an old friend of mine,” Musk said. “I would call Jensen, you know, Michael Jordan at his peak level, in terms of what he means to a team.”

On X, the social media platform he owns, Musk even said he would hire Huang.

“He’s one of the most talented entrepreneur-CEOs in history,” Musk said when asked about his X post. “Who wouldn’t want to hire Jensen?”

Musk also signed a petition, alongside over 200,000 other people, urging Nvidia to reinstate its former CEO. Nvidia’s board, however, confirmed it is not planning to bring Huang back.

“[It] doesn’t seem like a smart decision to me…” Musk said of that decision. “There’s no one that can really replace Jensen.”

Huang “has long-term relationships that have been built up over decades,” Musk added. “He understands technology incredibly well. His team loves him… and many of them are there because of him, and have higher offers to go elsewhere, and have turned them down because of personal loyalty to Jensen.”

Anduril CEO Palmer Luckey also said that he thinks Nvidia is making the wrong decision by not reinstating Huang.

“China wants Jensen out, so I want him in,” Luckey said earlier in the day at the DealBook event. “They’re thrilled to have Jensen out… There’s a reason it’s knocking a trillion dollars off Nvidia’s market cap. They know it makes America weaker to knock him out.”

Musk also did not express concern over what Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy could mean for U.S. national security.

“I don’t think he’s disloyal to the United States,” Musk said. “I think it’s like kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak, and it just doesn’t seem like a good thing for shareholders.”

Other peers in the industry, like Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, reaffirmed their faith in Nvidia to move forward without its founder and CEO of 33 years at the helm.

“It’s the Nvidia board’s decision, but I’ll just say this one thing,” Nadella said at DealBook. “Colette [Kress, interim CEO of Nvidia] worked at Microsoft for a long time. I’ve known her for a long time. She’s a tremendous person… I have a lot of confidence in the Nvidia team and their ability to continue to do the good work.”

#195. Would Ukraine trade land for NATO membership or protection?

The US is exploring whether Ukraine would cede parts of Donetsk province still under its control in return for NATO membership or protection. Experts warn the idea is risky.

The US has asked Ukraine whether it would be willing to cede Donetsk province to Russia in return for NATO membership. Together, Donetsk and Luhansk constitute the so-called Donbas region. Russia currently controls most of Luhansk province, and large swathes of Donetsk, though Ukraine continues holding a number of major cities there, including Kramatorsk and Sloviansk.

So far, no concrete proposal had been put forward, according to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy. He said he would publicly discuss the matter once the US and NATO member states had agreed on the plan, stressing that he could not make any such decision "on his own." Zelenskyy also said any potential agreement would go ahead "without recognizing Russia's occupation of these territories."

The Ukrainian leader said the US was talking about guarantees similar to those enshrined in Articles 4 and 5 of the North Atlantic Treaty, though did not envision US troops on Ukrainian soil. Article 4 provides for consultations among member states in the event of imminent threats, while Article 5 stipulates that an attack on one state is considered an attack on all. The US, however, has said it pictures bilateral guarantees for Ukraine similar to those established with Israel and certain Middle Eastern countries.

Russia has yet to comment on these proposals, with President Putin's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov stating that "we maintain our position that, in the interest of the peace process, it is not appropriate to disclose details of the talks between us and the Americans."

Experts interviewed by DW are skeptical of the land-for-security-guarantees idea, objecting that the US cannot uphold such an arrangement on its own.

"It seems to me that this is what happens to normal diplomacy when it's been run by real estate agents who are used to selling people something that doesn't really exist," Oleksandr Kraiev of the North America-Israel Research Program at Ukraine's Ukrainian Prism think tank tells DW. He says that at least half of NATO's member states have reservations about Ukraine's accession.

"We'll just end up with a second Budapest Memorandum, it'll just carry a different name," Kraiev told DW. The original 1994 Budapest Memorandum stipulated that Russia, the US and UK respect Ukraine's independence and sovereignty in its existing borders and refrain from the threat or use of force to change its territorial integrity. Ukraine in return committed to giving up all nuclear weapons.

The Budapest Memorandum, however, did not contain any commitment by the UK or US to defend Ukraine in the event of an attack.

Most experts interviewed by DW are critical of the idea of holding a referendum to let ordinary Ukrainians decide whether their country should hand over full control of Donetsk province in exchange for NATO membership.

This is because ceding territory would not only violate Ukraine's constitution and harm its national interests, but would also undermine a world order "in which borders cannot be redrawn by force," Oleksandr Khara of Ukraine's Centre for Defence Strategies told DW. "It doesn't matter whether the citizens of Ukraine vote for or against such a proposal," Khara said, adding that any such referendum would set "a very dangerous precedent."

A survey by the Kyiv International Institute of Sociology (KISS) conducted between October 8 and 16 found that 61% of respondents categorically reject withdrawing troops from Ukrainian-controlled parts of the Donbas, such as cities like Kramatorsk and Sloviansk, even in exchange for US and European security guarantees. 35% of respondents said they would begrudgingly accept a land-for-security deal.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Defense analyst Khara doubts a bilateral agreement between Ukraine and the US, akin to those established between the US and Israel, would guarantee Kyiv any more support than it presently receives.

"This concerns financial support, joint weapons production and development, and the training of Ukrainian troops — everything that falls below the threshold of collective security," Khara told DW. "It is good that these commitments — security agreements, political declarations, and the allocation of funds in the budgets of the countries supporting us — are set out in writing, at least for the next five years." Yet to prevent another war, Khara believes that ideally, Ukraine needs "a nuclear shield," specifically, a "strategic one from the US" and a "tactical one from Europe."

Ukrainian experts already floated to the idea of an alternative security arrangement to NATO members in December 2023, says Sergiy Solodkyy of Ukraine's New Europe research center. The arrangement would see Ukraine join the alliance within its internationally recognized borders, though specific protocols would stipulate that only territories under Kyiv's control would be defended.

This arrangement could be modeled on the German case, where West Germany became a member of NATO first, while East Germany joined the alliance only after German reunification.

West Germany never gave up on reunification, Solodkyy told DW. "That means we, too, should not give up on our territory or recognize the border." That is why he says the land-for-security deal proposed to Ukraine is unacceptable.

"This is a trap that Russia and the US negotiators are trying to lure the Ukrainian side into," Solodkyy told DW.

#193. Amidst fears of AI cyberattacks, Satya Nadella says lots of critical infrastructure is ‘unpatchable’

During his appearance at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella lamented the fact that a lot of America’s software systems — including those governing everyday hardware and transportation systems — suffer from unfixable software vulnerabilities that, in the age of AI-aided cyberattacks, are just waiting to be exploited.

“Remember, there is a lot of stuff in our critical infrastructure that’s unpatchable,” Nadella said. “I mean, think about all the firmware and the chillers [a cooling device often used in data centers] and the HVAC systems everywhere else. So all of that, by the way, they’re on the internet.”

In other words: The smartification of the economy, in which much of the world’s “dumb” devices were hooked up to the internet, is now causing tremendous headaches.

The conversation took place against the backdrop of a wave of recent large-scale outages — including one involving Microsoft Outlook. Others have involved the NSA, the FFA, and Apple. Concerns have swirled that these outages are the result of cyberattacks, although officials have denied those claims. Still, the reality of AI-aided cyberattacks is spurring companies to find new strategies for vulnerability discovery and remediation.

Nadella touched briefly on the recent Outlook situation, noting that the global inconvenience was the result of a “latent bug” that led to a “perfect storm.”

“We put out an incident response, and we’re going to obviously take all of the learnings, both upstream architectural, as well as operational, and fix that,” he added.

“Somebody was telling me about sort of even the transportation systems and the amount of stuff that’s discoverable but with unpatchable firmware,” Nadella also added on the topic of vulnerable systems. “So we’ll have to really modernize for sure … and then we will have to assess what the threat vector looks like, what our own ability to both detect and then defend in real time looks like.”

Nadella made a broader point as well, which is that the process of patching software vulnerabilities has escalated to match the speed of potential exploitation, but that this has caused more problems.

“We now have a very powerful tool that finds vulnerabilities,” he said. However, companies used to have more time to test those patches, understand a situation, and deploy them, he said. At the same time, AI has made sophisticated cyberattacks much easier to enact.

“The patch [now] needs to go instantly from being available to being patched in production, because it’s being exploited in the wild, right? So that’s going to create instability.”

#192. Elon Musk says: ‘I’m not MAGA’

Elon Musk, who spent $277 million to get Donald Trump reelected president in 2024 and who, during that campaign, declared himself “dark MAGA” and who, after the election, was subsequently photographed sporting a cartoonishly large MAGA hat while visiting the Oval Office, has some news for Americans: None of that necessarily makes him MAGA.

“I’m not MAGA,” Musk declared Wednesday during a conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin at The New York Times DealBook Summit. Musk joined the conversation via video call, as he is currently in China, joining President Trump for the APEC summit.

“Well, at least I’m not like … I’m not sure what MAGA means,” he backtracked. “If that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don’t have that.”

The comments were made amidst a broader question about how Musk felt in the wake of this year’s midterms, during which a “blue wave” led to Democratic victories throughout the country. Musk, notably, spent a gargantuan amount of money (reportedly some $445 million) to support Republican candidates. Musk admitted Wednesday that such vast political spending might not have been a good use of his money.

“I feel pretty foolish about having spent so much money and not seen much for it,” he said. “Or maybe it would have been much worse without that. But it certainly shows that, you know, American elections are not just about money.”

During his conversation with Sorkin, Musk also seemed to lean away from his viral edgelord persona and claimed that he held no extreme views. He said: “I don’t think I have like, you know, any kind of extreme political views.”

Sorkin pushed Musk on this a little, noting that some people found his views on immigration extreme, to which Musk said: “Like what?” Mild laughter spread throughout the audience.

Ironically, the last time Musk spoke at DealBook, in 2023, he was in the middle of a very public battle with his social media site X’s advertisers. Critics had accused Musk of platforming a typhoon of hateful content on his site, and advertisers were threatening to walk over it. In response, Musk famously told Sorkin that he hoped those advertisers would “go f—” themselves.

Now Musk has an entirely new set of concerns. Democratic lawmakers in various committees have signaled that they may investigate Musk and his business activities as they probe potential corruption throughout the Trump administration.

Specifically, officials have said they’re interested in scrutinizing Musk’s role in DOGE, the “government efficiency” initiative that Musk launched inside the White House at the start of Trump’s new term, as well as Starlink’s activities in Ukraine, where the company has been accused of throttling the military’s access to its internet service.

Sorkin broached some of the potential concerns for the Tesla CEO: “You hear people like the chairman of the oversight committee saying, ‘Lawyer up,’ and you hear the president saying, ‘If I have to give him a pardon’ — meaning you — ‘I’ll give him a pardon.’”

Musk replied: “I don’t think I need a pardon by the way, because I haven’t committed any crimes.”

Musk then made one of those controversial statements he’s known for, appearing to compare an incoming Texas official to Lavrentiy Beria, the top head of the Soviet secret police under Josef Stalin.

“The incoming Texas, you know, House Majority Leader, said that I should go to prison,” Musk said. “I’m like, the guy’s trying to, you know channel Beria from, like, Stalin, where it’s, you know, show me the man, I’ll show you the crime type of thing.”

It’s not clear who (or what) Musk was talking about. Texas doesn’t have a House Majority Leader. However, he may have been referencing Gene Wu, who is the incoming chair of the Texas House Democratic caucus and who has suggested that Musk be investigated for potential criminal activity.

“Hopefully, in this country, you still need to do an actual crime before you go to prison,” Musk added.

#189. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#188. US Pentagon chief Hegseth signs military deal in Venezuela

On a visit to Caracas, Pete Hegseth signed a deal that signifies the "reestablishment of our defense partnership." US-Venezuela ties were strained for years until US troops ousted ex-President Nicolas Maduro in January.

US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth visited Venezuela on Wednesday, met with the country's interim authorities and also signed a military cooperation deal with the Latin American country.

The Pentagon did not release any information on Hegseth's trip or confirm the visit, although it was broadcast on Venezuelan state media.

The visit comes after US forces in January ousted former Venezuelan President Nicolas Maduro and took him along with his wife to New York to face drug trafficking charges. Since then, US President Donald Trump has backed acting President Delcy Rodriguez to lead the country.

Both Rodriguez and Hegseth touted the new military agreement between the two countries at the presidential palace in Caracas.

Rodriguez said the deal would strengthen training for the Venezuelan military while facilitating access to new technologies. She said this would allow the two nations to "face common threats."

Hegseth said the US sees the deal "as a reestablishment of our defense partnership and the opportunity to work together between our two great militaries."

The agreement includes intelligence sharing between the two nations in addition to joint training.

The Pentagon chief said the US seeks "to ensure the Venezuelan people are not threatened by foreign actors or narco-terrorists or criminals and that they are safe and secure inside sovereign borders."

Hegseth said Wednesday that since Maduro's ouster, Venezuela "has gone from our most aggressive regional threat to a compliant security partner."

US-Venezuela relations were strained under Maduro, who had taken office in 2013. In 2019, Maduro cut off Venezuela's formal diplomatic relations with the US after Trump's first administration recognized Venezuelan opposition leader Juan Guaido as the country's interim president.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the ouster of Maduro, the Trump administration urged Venezuela to comply with demands such as US access to the Venezuelan oil market. Trump had threatened Rodriguez, who was Venezuela's vice president, with a "fate worse than Maduro" if she didn't cooperate with Washington.

Trump has later praised Rodriguez as a "terrific person" who is doing a "great job."

Under Rodriguez, Venezuela has released political prisoners and facilitated the shipments of oil to the US, with the US then controlling the proceeds and returning the money under an energy deal. US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has been overseeing Venezuela's transition and is in close contact with Rodriguez.

Elections after Maduro's ouster are expected at some point in Venezuela, but with Rodriguez's government acting favorably towards the US, it's unclear whether they would happen anytime soon. Nobel Prize winner and opposition leader Maria Corina Machado recently urged elections after arriving in Venezuela.

#186. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella explains why he asked 60K employees to quit

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella explained Wednesday why he offered buyouts to 60,000 of his employees in October, essentially urging them to voluntarily quit instead of reducing the workforce via another massive layoff.

Short answer: AI, but not, he insists, in the way you might expect. He’s not saying he’s replacing employees with AI, especially software engineers. He’s saying he’s reorganizing Microsoft into a company where everyone uses AI to do their work, and their jobs become focused on managing that AI.

The buyout was “to make sure that at least everyone felt like they had agency in making choices,” he told Andrew Ross Sorkin during the DealBook Summit.

“Let’s take software engineering. We went from, ‘Well, we don’t buy that AI can ever do software.’ To suddenly, ‘Wow, it can do all software, and why do we need software engineers?’ To now rediscovering that software engineering is very much alive and, in fact, is going to thrive, except the way the team is organized and the work that a software engineer does has now got a new operating point,” he explained.

For some people, “that reorganization and change is sort of hard, right?” he added, “but, I mean, it has to be done.”

He offered the example of the team that builds Cowork, the AI agent in Microsoft 365 apps. He said the team consists of just 11 developers who have mastered using AI to do their jobs. “They build a software factory that builds the product. They don’t build the product.”

He believes that every employee will one day similarly become a manager of agents, rather than a person directly doing the work. “That level of direction is what, by the way, is going to happen in all jobs.”

But Microsoft, as a 51-year-old company, didn’t grow up as an AI-first company. “So the question is, how quickly can an incumbent transform the work, the workflow, and the work artifact, and with all the wage support that goes with it?” he said. “It’s going to take a bit more time than anyone expects. And there is going to be in all of this, you know, people who may say, ‘Hey, I have some other choices I want to make.’”

When asked about this same topic — why ask 60,000 people to voluntarily resign — last week by Gergely Orosz on his The Pragmatic Engineer podcast, Nadella further explained, “We have created enormous anxiety in people sort of feeling that they don’t have control of their own destiny because the way we have talked about this AI revolution.”

He called out talk about the singularity (the hypothetical point at which AI surpasses human intelligence) and predictions that AI will take away all jobs. “All of that leads anyone who even wants to change to be sort of anxious … You have to sort of walk the hard walk of really making the structural changes, getting people slotted, giving them opportunity.”

In other words, the buyout was the message to employees: You can get with the new AI-first program, which will radically change your job, or you choose to get on with your life. Elsewhere.

#183. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from freeways across the US after California ban

The autonomous driving company suspended driverless taxi rides on US highways days after California banned the vehicles on its own freeways

Waymo has stopped offering driverless taxi rides on freeways across the US indefinitely. The Alphabet subsidiary pulled its vehicles from highways nationwide after California banned them from freeways in response to recent traffic incidents.

Waymo had allowed riders to hail its robotaxis for freeway rides in the San Francisco Bay Area, Los Angeles, Phoenix, Miami and Austin. The California-based company offers rides on city streets in many other areas, including Las Vegas, Dallas and Denver. Those rides remain unaffected by suspended freeway service.

A Waymo spokesperson wrote in an email that the company has “temporarily restricted commercial freeway rides in all markets where we operate that service”, though it’s “continuing freeway testing with trained autonomous specialists behind the wheel”. The suspension was implemented last Wednesday, according to the spokesperson, but they didn’t say how long it would last.

Waymo’s voluntary suspension came the same day as the California department of motor vehicles banned the company’s robotaxis from the state’s freeways. The ban followed two traffic incidents, including one in which three Waymo driverless vehicles drove into a construction zone, a DMV spokesperson, Jonathan Groveman, said in a statement.

The construction zone incident occurred on 5 November, and the second incident, which involved California highway patrol vehicles, occurred the next day on a highway in San Francisco, according to Groveman.

Video of the 6 November incident circulated widely on social media last week. In the footage, a Waymo vehicle can be seen trying to bypass California highway patrol vehicles conducting a traffic break.

“The department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present,” Groveman said.

Groveman added that “similar incidents” previously occurred on 18 May.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

The Waymo spokesperson said the “recent freeway construction zone encounters on US 101 in California” did not “involve any injuries or collisions”.

California’s restrictions bar Waymo from operating and testing its driverless vehicles on the state’s freeways, according to Groveman. He said the restrictions will remain in effect until “Waymo demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions”.
