#180. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#179. What the Crimean bridge attack means for Russia

Ukraine's latest strike on the Kerch Bridge has disrupted a key transport link between Russia and Crimea. Beyond the damage, the attack could have serious consequences for Russian military logistics and Putin's prestige.

It had been a year and a half since Ukraine last attacked the 19-kilometer-long Kerch Bridge connecting Russian-annexed Crimea with Russia's Krasnodar region.

Now, Ukraine has struck again. The attack during the night of November 17 caused the highway section of the bridge to collapse into the sea. As a result, shipping traffic between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov has also been disrupted.

Russia's ministry of transport announced a "temporary suspension" of road and rail traffic on the bridge, though later, rail services were resumed. Russian authorities have launched a criminal investigation into what they are calling a "terrorist attack." It was only after this announcement that the Security Service of Ukraine (SBU) confirmed the attack.

The Kerch Strait bridge is considered one of Russian President Vladimir Putin's most prestigious projects. Its construction began after Russia's 2014 annexation of the Ukrainian Crimea peninsula, an act considered illegal under international law. The Kerch Bridge was completed four years later.

It has been targeted by Ukraine on several occasions since Russia's 2022 full-scale invasion. The most well-known strike occurred in October 2022, when an explosives-laden truck detonated on the bridge, causing extensive damage and several months of traffic restrictions. The most recent attack took place in June 2025, when the SBU reported that an underwater support structure was blown up.

The latest Ukrainian operation lasted about a month, and consisted of seven distinct phases. "First, the SBU and armed forces systematically neutralized the enemy's air defense system. After that, the direct attack on the bridge began," says the SBU Telegram channel.

Some Russian military bloggers claim that the attack was carried out with the support of Ukraine's Western allies, or at least using their equipment and weapons.

Ukraine has not confirmed this. It has instead pointed out that various Ukrainian-made weapons were used in the attack, ranging from a variety of drones, to FP-5 Flamingo and R-360 Neptun missiles. British daily The Times has cited Ukrainian military sources, saying that Ukraine attacked the bridge without the support of SpaceX's satellite network system Starlink. There has been no confirmation of this so far.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, meanwhile, described the strike as a response to Russian attacks: "Russia is attacking our infrastructure every day, particularly bridges in Zaporizhzhia, Dnipro, Kyiv, and the Odesa region." He said "we are rebuilding our infrastructure, but we also remind Russia that the war always returns to where it began."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the attack, pictures and videos of the bridge were shared on social media. The Krimskij Veter Telegram channel has highlighted damage to the railroad link and questioned why Russian authorities have opted to resume train services.

The Kerch Strait shipping channel is also completely blocked by debris. "This brings road traffic to a standstill, but also shipping between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov," the channel writes.

Indeed, the Kerch Strait navigational channel is merely 120 meters wide, writes Mychajlo Hontschar of the Ukrainian Center for Global Studies "Strategy XXI" on Facebook. "The strait is shallow, only three to five meters deep."

As early as the 19th century, the seabed was dredged to allow heavier ships to pass from the Black Sea to the Sea of Azov and vice versa. "Now, Vladimir Putin's bridge-building efforts have brought shipping to an indefinite standstill," Hontschar said.

Not only will river-sea vessels no longer be able to leave the waters of the Sea of Azov, the Don, and the Volga, but boats and small ships from the Black Sea fleet and the Caspian flotilla based in Novorossiysk will also be unable to do so.

Many pro-Kremlin Telegram channels have been quick to claim that the bridge does not play an important role in the Russian army's logistics or in supplying Crimea.

The Dva Mayora (Two Majors) channel quotes the "Deputy President of the Republic of Crimea," Olga Kurlayeva, who described the bridge as a "purely civilian structure."

Ukrainian military experts interviewed by DW, on the other hand, point to the systematic disruption of Russian logistics. These efforts also included regular attacks on the R-80 highway leading through Russian-occupied territories of the Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson regions.

Russia cannot replace the Kerch Bridge with any other land connection, says Oleksandr Kovalenko, a Ukrainian military expert with the Information Resistance Group that seeks to counter disinformation. He expects the damaged bridge will cause supply problems for the Russian armed forces: "If there is a resource shortage during attacks or in defense, then either the attacks or the defense will fail."

That is why he does not rule out a decline in attacks from occupied Crimea on the rest of Ukraine. "There are many launch pads for kamikaze drones in Crimea — in the Hvardijsk area, at Cape Chauda and in Balaklava," Kovalenko told DW. "From there, many drones are launched toward Odesa, Mykolaiv, and the right bank of the Dnipro River in the Kherson region. They also fly over Crimea en route toward Ukraine. Then there are attacks using Iskander-M ballistic missiles, as well as Zircon and Oniks missiles, guided missiles, and glide bombs. And while they don't originate in Crimea, they are brought over via the Kerch Bridge."

Kovalenko says the bridge strike has dealt a serious blow to Vladimir Putin's standing. It comes at a sensitive time as Putin is about to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and US President Donald Trump in Shenzhen, China.

Memes and witty comments about the bridge abound on X. Meanwhile, the Russian president's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has promised to rebuild the bridge and assured that it will be "even better than before."

Andrii Korezkyj of the Kyiv University of Transportation, however, says this is not likely to happen anytime soon. "All in all, I would estimate that it would take between one and a half and three years [to rebuild]," Korezkyj told DW, adding, "I don't think they'll let the Russians build another [bridge] arch like that near the Ukrainian border without interference."

Pro-Russian commentators, known as Z-bloggers, agree with this view. "Unlike in 2022 and 2023, the Ukrainian armed forces are now able to launch strikes against floating cranes and repair teams."

"Even installing a pre-assembled arch under these conditions would constitute a challenging task," writes the pro-Kremlin Rybar Telegram channel.

#177. Record-breaking rain lashes California’s Bay area as ‘atmospheric river’ arrives

Storm expected to move into southern California, with LA residents warned of potential evacuation orders

Heavy rain pounded California’s Bay area, with record rainfall in downtown San Francisco and flooding and power outages across the region, as an “atmospheric river” arrived on Wednesday and dumped precipitation across the state.

The storm was expected to move into southern California on Wednesday night and Thursday, with Los Angeles warning residents in mudslide-prone areas of potential evacuation orders to come. Southern Californians were also warned of potential coastal flooding, heavy surf, wave damage to coastal piers and parking lots and beach sand erosion. Heavy snow warnings were issued for higher elevations of the Sierra Mountains, including in the Greater Lake Tahoe area.

Downtown San Francisco saw the most rainfall before noon in 76 years, at 2.11in, surpassing a record from 1950, the San Francisco Chronicle reported. By 3pm, Wednesday’s daily total of 2.28in had broken the record for the most rainfall on 18 November, which was previously set in 1918, the National Weather Service reported. South of San Francisco, in Santa Clara, there was flooding and standing water on several highways.

In San Jose, the local fire department posted on social media that two vehicles with passengers inside had become stranded in the flooded streets downtown near the SAP Center, the city’s major events arena. The second passenger was being extricated via a boat rescue, the department wrote.

Daniel Lurie, the San Francisco mayor, warned city residents the region’s winter storm and flood season, supercharged by this year’s very strong El Niño weather, was just getting started.

Despite the Wednesday afternoon break in the rain, he posted on social media, “The storm should pick back up tonight, and more rain is in the forecast for next week.”

“When these heavy storms come, we expect flooding to return to many of the same areas that were impacted this morning,” he added.

“Today’s storm certainly gave the Bay Area a good assessment of our pain points for future storms this season,” Drew Tuma, a local meteorologist for ABC7 News, wrote on social media.

Street flooding in San Francisco left maintenance hole covers floating in one neighborhood of San Francisco, while the self-driving car company Waymo temporarily suspended service in the city, the Chronicle reported. One Waymo stalled in a flooded street and blocked traffic, ABC7 News reported.

An overwhelmed sewer stormwater system also appeared to result in several “sewage geysers”, one reportedly 15-ft tall, the Chronicle reported, sharing bystander footage of the spew in the city’s Ocean Beach neighborhood.

Thousands across the Bay area were dealing with storm-related power outages by Wednesday afternoon, according to a customer outage map from Pacific Gas & Electric, the major utility company for much of northern California.

In Monterey, south of San Francisco, the local aquarium posted on social media that “storms and massive waves are causing a surge of sea otter strandings this week in Monterey Bay” and that their staff “safely rescued several pups” after receiving calls from local community members.

Areas of the state still recovering from recent wildfires continue to be at risk for flash flooding, with specific warnings issued for “burn scar” areas including in Big Sur and the Napa valley.

In Los Angeles, city officials warned residents of areas burned by the 2025 Palisades, Sunset and Hurst fires, north of the city, to be prepared for potential evacuation orders starting on Wednesday night, as heavy rains brought increased risk of flooding, mudslide and debris flow.

Statewide, the heaviest of the rainfall was expected north-east of San Francisco, from Santa Rosa to Chico and Redding in the state’s Central valley, and in the southern part of the Bay Area, along coastal mountain ranges from Santa Cruz to San Jose and Palo Alto, according to the National Weather Service.

“Travel delays, localized flooding and hazardous conditions are possible, especially along the coast and on mountain pass roads,” AccuWeather warned in an online forecast.

Atmospheric rivers, massive currents of water vapor that can carry as much or even more water than an actual river, have been growing stronger and deadlier in recent years. In the relatively dry western US, atmospheric river storms provide both essential precipitation and flood risk.

In southern California, the NWS predicted 1-3in of rain in many areas, with 3in-5in in some mountain and foothill areas, and a 20% chance of thunderstorms on Wednesday and Thursday. As much as a foot of snow was expected in higher-elevation areas above 6,000ft. There was a high risk of “significant road flooding” and a moderate risk for rockslides and mudslides, the NWS said.

The risk of coastal wave damage was expected to peak in southern California on Thursday, with additional risks expected next week, with seasonal high tides overlapping with storm risks during Thanksgiving week.

Waterspouts were spotted over the San Francisco Bay earlier this week, and the NWS flagged the risk of additional waterspouts over the ocean east of San Francisco on Wednesday.

#174. Elon Musk claims ‘I’m not Maga’ and says he regrets vast sums to back Republicans

Musk, who aligned with Trump and was frequent White House guest, says he feels ‘pretty foolish’ about record midterm spending

Elon Musk denied being Maga on Wednesday, despite aligning himself intensely with Donald Trump’s Make America Great Again movement since 2024.

“I’m not Maga,” the world’s richest person told the New York Times Dealbook summit, which he attended via video call from China, where he is one of several US business leaders who have accompanied Trump on his trip to Asia.

“I’m not sure what Maga means, but if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican party, then I certainly don’t have that,” the chief executive of SpaceX and Tesla added.

Musk famously declared at a 2024 campaign rally in Madison Square Garden: “I’m not just Maga. I’m dark, gothic Maga.”

In a 45-minute interview with Andrew Ross Sorkin, a New York Times columnist, Musk also said it would be good for Washington to have split control between the two parties, and that he thinks “there are positives and negatives in both”.

He also accused Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, of “lying” about restrictions on access to his firm’s Starshield satellite system; praised Jensen Huang, the ousted Nvidia chief executive; and said that “no one I met at Anthropic set off my evil detector”.

Asked about his previous negative comments about Anthropic, Musk said that before he agreed to rent computing power to the AI firm: “I went to met with a lot of people at Anthropic. I didn’t just move forward with the deal.”

“My interaction so far with the Anthropic team is that they seem like good people and they seem principled,” Musk added. “They seem diligent and they seem seem to care about the future.”

Musk donated at least $250m of his own money to help Trump win the election in 2024, and recently spent nearly $500m in the US midterms to support Republicans, many of whom lost.

Musk admitted at the Dealbook summit that his spending had “failed”, and that he felt “pretty foolish” for spending such a sum, the most that anyone has ever spent in a political cycle when they themselves did not run for office. “It certainly shows that American elections are not just about money,” he said. “If I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money.”

After Trump took office for the second time in 2025, Musk served for 180 days heading the so-called “department of government efficiency” (Doge) within Trump’s administration, before stepping down following a very public falling out with the president.

That culminated in Musk posting on his social media platform in June of that year: “Time to drop the really big bomb: @realDonaldTrump is in the Epstein files. That is the real reason they have not been made public.”

The two men later reconciled. During his time leading Doge, Musk frequently appeared in the Oval Office and at cabinet meetings alongside the president, often sporting hats that read “Make America Great Again” or “Trump was right about everything”.

#171. Trump downplays US outages and Nvidia security concerns in remarks before China summit – as it happened

Follow our live coverage of the Asia-Pacific economic cooperation summit

Speaking with Fox news, Donald Trump said he wasn’t worried about multiple outages this week affecting Microsoft, US banks, United Airlines computers, the National Security Agency’s internal comms network, Apple services and the national air traffic control system.

“They’re all individual events. These are big companies. They’re putting in new equipment and things happen when you put in new equipment. And these are all individual things,” he said.

When asked whether he raised the issue of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman suspected of being a spy for China with Xi, Trump said he “might’ve referred to it just briefly”

“Because it’s such a tragedy. It’s a tragedy. He’s such a brilliant man ... we have to take care of our geniuses,” Trump said of Huang.

Of Alice Huang, Jensen Huang’s affair partner, the president mused: “I haven’t heard that she was a spy. She was a business woman who bought chips. Who knows if they were bought right or wrong. But I can tell you we allow them to buy. We want to make money.”

However, according to a report by the New York Times, Trump was briefed aboard Air Force One in May en route to China that the US intelligence showed that that Nvidia’s chief executive was in a relationship with a woman thought to be a Chinese spy.

Trump also shrugged off the Fox reporter’s question about whether he would confront China over spying allegations.

“We spy on them too”, he said. “I think we spy better than they do.”

A historic trilateral meeting is taking place in China between US president Donald Trump, China’s Xi Jinping and Russia’s Vladimir Putin. The leaders will be discussing Russia’s war with Ukraine, the US war in Iran, as well as global trade, AI, and nuclear weapons. Plus, the subject of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang being embroiled in a spy scandal has only come up “briefly” so far between Trump and Xi Jinping.

House Democrats have nominated current minority leader Hakeem Jeffries as their candidate for speaker in the next Congress. Jeffries received 251 yes votes of 262 cast, and 11 members voted no on his nomination. He faced no challengers but it will lead to a full chamber vote in the new year when Democrats are in the majority. Jeffries is poised to be the first Black speaker in US history.

During a fiery House hearing, Markwayne Mullin, the homeland security secretary, defended his decision to take a government plane to attend a college football game, while thousands of flights had been cancelled and the airspace closed during the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) nationwide grounding over the weekend. “You want to bring up the fact that I took my family and my great nieces to an [Lousiana State University] game that was scheduled for weeks prior because I wanted to be a good uncle,” Mullin said, following questions from Congressman Bennie Thompson, the homeland security committee’s top Democrat. Mullin went further, and said that had he not traveled to the game it could have created “confusion” because he was due to meet Louisiana governor Jeff Landry. Mullin added that his absence might have shown “distrust that we’re not even willing to fly” after the FAA reopened the airspace.

Elon Musk called his midterm political spending “foolish.” After pouring nearly half a billion dollars into the election, in a Wednesday conversation with The New York Times’s DealBook Summit, Musk said: “If I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money.”

US Transportation secretary Sean Duffy blamed contractor L3Harris for last week’s nationwide ground stop. As legislators press for more answers on last week’s outage that caused thousands of flights to be halted, Duffy has continued finger-pointing, telling digital media outlet 2WAY on Wednesday that L3Harris, the aerospace and defense company whose router malfunctioned during an update, did not identify the issue “quick enough.”

Trump’s contentious triumphal arch continues to face setbacks. A federal judge extended the construction ban for the arch, slated for the Memorial Circle in Washington DC. A group of veterans and members of the Kennedy family members have blasted the project as disrespectful to those interred at the Arlington national cemetery.

Alaska Republican representative Nick Begich has retained his seat, according to the Associated Press. The call was made Wednesday evening after the state’s ranked choice tabulation, a process that occurs when candidates do not receive over 50% of the vote.

Ultimately, Begich, a Trump-backed millionaire, secured 51.35% of the vote, besting his independent challenger Bill Hill, a fisherman who ran on a message of affordability, as Alaskans weather soaring energy prices. Hill received 48.65%.

The race also included a Democrat, Eric Hafner, an apparent spoiler candidate, who advanced in the primary, despite being incarcerated in federal prison.

Hill said on X that he called Begich to congratulate his opponent on the win.

“This campaign has always been about taking power back from DC elites and putting it in the hands of the hardworking people of Alaska,” he wrote. “While we did not prevail in this election, we will continue that fight together.”

US president Donald Trump, China’s Xi Jinping and Russia’s Vladimir Putin are set to engage in trilateral talks, a blockbuster diplomatic event for the three countries.

On the agenda is Russia’s war with Ukraine, the US war in Iran, as well as global trade, AI, and nuclear weapons.

Trump arrived at the Asia-Pacific economic corporation (Apec) meeting in Shenzhen, China on Thursday morning local time, pausing for photographs on a red carpet, the only leader to do so.

Member states of Apec took a family photo, as they donned red and blue silk scarves.

Donald Trump, who repeatedly prophesied an apocalyptic future if Democrats regained control of Congress, has changed his tune, telling Fox News the next two years will be “unbelievably productive.”

He suggested he would be liberal with his veto power, but also open to cutting deals across the party line.

“They’ll send me a bill on health care and I’ll say no way and I’ll veto it. And then, they’ll call me and we’ll sit down. That’s what I do,” Trump said on Wednesday. “Politics is a nasty game. They won.”

Donald Trump will reportedly meet one-on-one with Vladimir Putin, the prime minister of Russia, during his Asia summit.

When asked by Fox News if he mentioned the bilateral meeting to Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy, Trump appeared to sidestep the question.

“They got to get along,” Trump replied, referring to Zelenskyy and Putin, whose countries remain at war. “If I were president, if the election weren’t rigged, that war would’ve never happened,” he added, repeating a baseless election fraud theory.

Since February 2022, when Putin ordered the full-scale invasion of Ukraine by Russian forces, nearly 18,000 citizens have been killed and 58,000 injured, according to estimates from the United Nations.

Trump then offered a wildly inflated death toll for the conflict, claiming that “probably two million people, mostly soldiers, are dead.” In fact, more than 400,000 Russians have been killed fighting in Ukraine since Russia’s full-scale invasion, according to leaked data from the state statistics agency, Rosstat. In July, the Center for Strategic and International Studies estimated that between 125,000 and 150,000 Ukrainian soldiers have been killed.

“It’s a shame. It’s so sad. It doesn’t affect us,” Trump told Fox News. He noted that the US has sold military equipment to the European Union, which provides it to Ukrainian forces, in contrast to what he framed as handouts from his predecessor Joe Biden’s administration. Trump repeated his false claim that “Biden gave $350m in cash away” to Ukraine. In fact, the US Congress provided about half that sum, $174bn, in arms to Ukraine, and the Biden administration gave Ukraine a $20 billion loan to be repaid with frozen Russian assets.

“I guess you could say, we make a lot of money with that. I don’t care. I don’t want that,” Trump said.

US authorities have admitted altering the translation of an extradition request for James “Fergie” Chambers from English into Spanish, attributing violent and illegal actions to the wealthy activist and philanthropist that were not in the original.

Chambers’s case is the first known instance of the US seeking to extradite a citizen over alleged support for Hamas, an accusation apparently tied to an international money-laundering charge in the criminal complaint underpinning the extradition request and donations to organizations helping Palestinians in Gaza.

In a notification sent to Spain’s ministry of justice, the US Department of Justice (DoJ) cites three of the examples of legally significant alterations revealed in the Guardian’s reporting on the issue, calling them “substantial [and] inadvertent”.

The notification arrived to defense attorneys, the prosecutor and a three-judge panel on Tuesday as the judges were deliberating whether to grant the US extradition request, leading the defense team to file a motion calling for a halt in the proceedings and a new translation.

The US has now admitted to three of the alterations detailed in the Guardian’s reporting, and calls the fourth “an acceptable translation”. One of them involves introducing the word “fire” in describing late 2023 protests against Elbit, an Israeli weapons manufacturer. Two of the three charges in the criminal complaint against Chambers are related to the protests – where nothing was set on fire.

A federal judge has extended the construction ban on Trump’s triumphal arch, in the latest setback for the president’s capital makeover agenda.

The Memorial Circle-slated arch was conceived as a visual ode to the nation’s 250th year of independence, among several other beautification projects, but its construction has been halted amid litigation.

In February, a trio of veterans and an architectural historian filed a legal challenge against the Trump administration, saying the arch would obstruct the symbolic view between the Arlington national cemetery and the Lincoln memorial, a line of sight meant to represent a unified nation post civil war, according to the complaint.

Members of the Kennedy family have since joined the legal action, arguing the arch would cast a shadow on former president John F. Kennedy and other relatives’s graves, which US district judge Tanya Chutkan cited in her extension.

The order is set to expire on 3 December, or before if the court rules on pending motions for a preliminary injunction.

Elon Musk poured nearly half a billion dollars into the midterms, as he sought to tilt competitive races in favor of Republican candidates.

In the end, Democrats claimed victory across the country, including in Texas where Musk’s aerospace and rocket company SpaceX is headquartered.

“It certainly shows that American elections are not just about money,” Musk said on Wednesday at the New York Times DealBook Summit according to the paper.

“If I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money.”

As legislators press for more answers on last week’s outage that caused thousands of flights to be halted, snarling travel plans nationwide and globally, US transportation secretary Sean Duffy has pointed a finger at contractor L3Harris.

Transportation officials have said the aerospace and defense company was updating copper wire lines to fiber in Arkansas, when a newly installed router flooded the network.

“This was not identified quick enough. It was not isolated quick enough,” Duffy said Wednesday on digital media outlet 2WAY’s The Morning Meeting. “We’re working on how to remedy that right now.”

A spokesperson for the government contractor could not be immediately reached for a response.

The official cause of the router malfunction remains unknown.

Duffy’s agency has been modernizing an outdated air traffic control system, switching copper lines to fiber and wireless, and analog systems to digital. Last Friday’s ground stop order was the second advisory of its type, since the 9/11 attacks.

In its wake, Duffy has also tore into Democratic lawmakers and previous administrations over funding to the aging system.

#169. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#167. Grand Theft Auto VI launches to global fanfare

After 13 years of waiting, fans around the world will get to play the latest installment in the blockbuster video game series. In focus: A tale of crime and violence in a fictionalized version of Miami.

Grand Theft Auto VI officially launched Thursday, ending a 13-year wait for one of the most anticipated games ever.

The game became playable at midnight local time, depending on location, on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X, meaning gamers in Asia will get to play the game ahead of users in the US and Europe.

In New Zealand, one of the first countries worldwide where the game became available, the release prompted internet providers to prepare for a spike in internet usage at midnight.

This is due to the game's size of 212 gigabytes, more than a quarter of the average New Zealand household's usage in a month.

Rockstar Games, the company behind the GTA franchise, faces high expectationsn after 13 years of anticipation, with previous versions having become commercial and cultural hits around the world.

GTA games sold 470 million units worldwide, and the fifth installment generated $1 billion in retail sales in just three days after its release in 2013.

Take-Two Interactive, Rockstar Games' parent company, saw its shares rise by more than eight percent the day before the game's release.

The game takes place in Vice City, a fictional version of Miami.

It follows Lucia Caminos and Jason Duval, a couple drawn into criminal networks while trying to secure a better life. GTA games are often criticized for including excessive violence and for glorifying crimes and drug use.

Lucia, a former prisoner, and Jason, an ex-military man, experience a series of criminal jobs that go wrong in the drug-trafficking world of Leonida, a fictional state based on Florida.

According to Rockstar, Leonida is the company's most detailed open world so far, extending beyond Vice City into smaller communities and nightlife hotspots inspired by locations in South Florida.

Music has been an intrinsic part of the GTA experience, and the latest game is expected to feature a soundtrack including artists like Tame Impala and SZA.

#166. Hannah Lillith Assadi wins National book award for ‘visionary’ novel Paradiso 17

Assadi’s third novel was inspired by the story of her late father, a Palestinian who escaped the Nakba as a child in 1948

Palestinian-Jewish author Hannah Lillith Assadi has won the National book award for fiction, one of the US’s most prestigious literary prizes, for her novel Paradiso 17.

Assadi’s third novel is a study of the psychic toll of displacement on migrants, through the tale of a Palestinian man who escaped the Nakba in 1948, when more than 700,000 Palestinians fled or were expelled from their homes by Israel. The story was inspired by her father, Sami Abdul-Fattah Al-Assadi, who fled the Nakba as a child with his family, before studying in Italy and moving to New York.

In a tearful speech, Assadi said, “What a beautiful story this has been, from its origins at my father’s deathbed until now, ending here and accepting this award. And so full of pain.

“In the course of its writing, I have experienced deep personal grief and unimaginable collective grief. I have watched cancer ravage my father’s body and genocide ravage his people and their children. And perhaps this is the Palestinian story on this earth. So beautiful. So painful.”

The judges praised Assadi’s “elegant lyricism” and “ambitious bending of time and space” and called the novel “a visionary, finely wrought portrait of rupture and endurance”.

Assadi won the $10,000 prize against Edwidge Danticat’s Dèy, Julián Delgado Lopera’s Pretend You’re Dead and I Carry You, Valeria Luiselli’s Beginning Middle End and Morgan Thomas’ Mad Eden.

Egyptian author Abdelrahman ElGendy won the $10,000 nonfiction prize for his debut Huna: A Memoir of Revolution, Prison, and Becoming, about his six years as a political prisoner in Egypt. ElGendy was unable to attend the ceremony after being denied a visa to the US, so his prize was collected by his editor Helen Thomaides, who read out his speech.

ElGendy said he had dreamed of turning his experience into a memoir as it gave him distance from the physical abuse he endured, and revealed he had practised award acceptance speeches under his duvet in prison.

“Today that dream should have come full circle but, like so many of the circles ruptured in my life, it will remain unfinished, broken by an oppressive regime with a fascist ideology whose concentration camps stand ready to swallow the bodies it deems undesirable,” ElGendy wrote.

The New York Times speculated earlier this month that ElGendy was not even eligible for the award as he did not meet its citizenship or residency requirements after fleeing the US due to Ice deportations, but the National Book Foundation, which administers the National book Awards, disputed this and said it did not “have any concerns regarding the eligibility of his title”.

Samar Yazbek of Syria, a finalist for the books in translation category, was also denied a visa, with the National Book Foundation describing both decisions as “unjust”.

The state department declined to comment on any specific cases, but issued a statement to Associated Press saying that “the Trump Administration continues to put America and its interests first by fully enforcing US immigration laws and ensuring lawful travel through our visa process. As Secretary [of State Marco] Rubio said, ‘a visa is a privilege, not a right’.”

Publishers submitted 1,912 books to the National book awards this year, with the winners judged by panels of authors, editors, booksellers and others in the literary community.

Brenda Hillman won the $10,000 poetry category for Still House in the Desert: An eco-contemplation, praised as “a rigorously unsentimental portrait of the stillness … of the poet’s childhood home after her mother’s death”.

David Leviathan and Gabriel Duckels won the $10,000 young people’s literature category for The Fight of Our Lives: AIDS in America.

Indian-British author Salman Rushdie was presented with a medal for “distinguished contribution to American letters”, while paediatrician and author Perri Klass was honoured for “outstanding service to the American literary community”.

Rushdie recalled being welcomed into the US literary scene after spending years under police protection in London after the fatwa over The Satanic Verses, and used his speech to thank the late Ursula K Le Guin for liking his first novel. “She was just about the only one who did! And it meant everything to me,” he said, to laughs.

“All I want to say is that it has been quite a ride, from over there to over here,” he added. “And to ride into this town and end up in this saloon, with these companions, is a hell of a thing.”

Actor and author Kyle MacLachlan hosted the ceremony, held at Cipriani Wall Street, while singer Michelle Zauner, author of the bestselling memoir Crying in H Mart, performed onstage.

#164. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#162. Leaseholders in England and Wales to have ground rent capped in 2028

Housing minister hails ‘historic moment’ as he publishes bill to eventually ban almost all new leasehold properties

Leaseholders in England and Wales will have their ground rent capped in 2028, the housing minister has said as he publishes a bill that will eventually ban virtually all new leasehold properties.

Matthew Pennycook will introduce the government’s leasehold reform bill on Thursday, including measures to cap charges, end the sale of new leasehold flats and make it easier to extend and buy out leases.

He said one of the first elements of the bill to come into force would be the long-promised £250 cap on annual ground rent – though it will not be enacted as quickly as some MPs have pushed for.

“The ground rent can be expected fairly early,” he said. “I think it will be a really historic moment … leasehold denies individuals control over the homes they live in and have paid for.

“It then allows freeholders and managing agents acting on their behalf to extract excessive amounts of money from them, whether that’s unreasonable or extortionate charges, punitive and escalating ground rents, unjustified administration and permission fees – you name it.

“Leasehold is an anachronism in the 21st century. I really do think that the bill that we’re introducing tomorrow [Thursday] marks the beginning of the end for the feudal leasehold system.”

Pennycook and his Conservative predecessors promised to end the leasehold system, which exists in only a few countries worldwide and dates back to feudal property laws.

Thursday’s bill will mark the biggest step towards making those promises a reality, extending the ban on leasehold for new houses to flats, which make up the bulk of leasehold properties. Pennycook has previously said he expects the ban to come into force after the next election.

It will also make it easier for those in leasehold properties to switch to a collective system known as commonhold, ensuring that such a move can be made with the agreement of half the residents in a block rather than all of them.

And it will end freeholders’ right to evict leaseholders for relatively small unpaid bills.

One of the first changes to become law will be the £250 cap on ground rent.

MPs on the housing select committee had urged Pennycook to introduce it as soon as next year, but he insisted it was not possible because of the “technical process” of beginning its enforcement. The 2028 date would not be explicitly included in the bill but ministers would enact it through secondary legislation, he said.

He also said many other leaseholders’ demands would have to come in a second law to be introduced later in the parliament.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

They include establishing an independent regulator for property management agents and changing the rates that determine how much it costs to extend or buy out a lease.

Ministers are still considering proposals, meanwhile, which would strip out the costs of prospective future development from the cost of a lease.

Pennycook said that wrestling with the legal technicalities of property law had slowed the process of drafting the bill, arguing that doing so had minimised the risk of the government having to go back and rewrite parts of it.

“This is by far and away the most complex and technical piece of legislation I have ever dealt with,” he said.

“We are undertaking the most ambitious overhaul of land and property law in a century, if not longer … it is just ferociously complex.

“We’re taking on some big vested interests, and those vested interests have very deep pockets.

“Leasehold law is extremely litigious – we’ve had challenges brought to the 2024 Act … [and] we will get challenges to this piece of legislation as well, almost certainly.”

#161. Weight-loss drugs used in nearly one in 10 homes in Great Britain, study finds

About 2.6m adults are taking the drugs, fuelled by the regulator’s approval of the pill versions and a rise in male users

At least one person is taking weight-loss drugs in 8.5% of households in Great Britain, according to research, meaning there are more homes with GLP-1 users than those containing vegetarians and pescatarians combined.

About 2.6 million adults were using weight-loss medication in August, a rise of about 700,000 people since February, according to a survey by Worldpanel by Numerator.

In February 6.3% of households had someone using GLP-1 drugs, rising to 8.5% in August. Weight-loss pills such as Wegovy were approved in the UK in June. Patients previously had to use the injectable version.

Pills now account for more than one in 10 (11.2%) of GLP-1 users, while men are also driving the increase. Men make up just under a third (32%) of people taking weight-loss medication, up from 23% in February. Male users have nearly doubled to 830,000, up from 440,000 nine months ago.

The increased use of medications such as Mounjaro and Wegovy is causing people to buy less food at the supermarket and change their buying habits, according to a survey of more than 9,600 households.

In the year after starting to take the drugs, households with a GLP‑1 user collectively spent £1bn less on groceries than expected compared with similar households. In June, Worldpanel calculated the total saved by households with a GLP-1 user was £780m.

This represents 415m fewer items bought, or £389 a year displaced from the shopping baskets of households with a GLP-1 user, according to the research by Worldpanel, which has been tracking the effects of the drugs on buying behaviour since March 2024.

Nishita Pattni, a senior consultant for Worldpanel by Numerator, said: “In just six months, between February and August 2026, user numbers rose almost as much as they did in the whole of the previous year.

“For many people, a daily pill is far easier than an injection, and with the surge in male users, we only expect the appeal of these drugs to grow.”

GLP-1 users are now found in a similar percentage of households in Great Britain as those that buy popular branded products such as Kellogg’s Rice Krispies (8.6%) and Corona beer (8.4%).

People taking weight-loss medication were not simply buying fewer groceries, the research found. Spend and volume were falling at a similar rate, creating winners and losers.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Products associated with indulgence, as well as impulse purchases, have taken the biggest hit.

Alcohol spend has fallen by 15% more among GLP-1 users than in households where no one uses GLP-1, while the spend on crisps has fallen by a similar percentage, as users tend to snack less.

Conversely, portion-controlled products are growing in popularity. There has been a 30% rise in the number of frozen ready-meals sold, and an increase in the volume and spend on hot chocolate and malt drinks as people who take weight-loss drugs continue to enjoy small treats.

Chantel Kennaugh, the head of public sector and nutrition for Worldpanel by Numerator, said estimates suggest that GLP-1 usage is likely to surpass 10% of households in Great Britain in 2027. “This is not a flash-in-the-pan trend, but a seismic change that requires retailers and manufacturers to rethink their strategies.”

#158. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Two women arrested for not wearing hijab following ‘yogurt attack’

Two women were arrested in Iran for failing to wear the hijab in public, after a man threw a tub of yogurt at them at a store in the city of Shandiz on Thursday, according to Mizan News Agency, the state-run outlet for Iran’s judiciary.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Oil prices spiked Monday after OPEC+ producers unexpectedly announced that they would cut output. Brent crude, the global benchmark, jumped 5.31% to $84.13 a barrel, while WTI, the US benchmark, rose 5.48% to $79.83. Both were the sharpest price rises in almost a year. The collective output cut by the nine members of OPEC+ totals 1.66 million barrels per day.

Iran blames Israel for the killing of second IRGC officer, vows to respond

A second Iranian Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) officer died following an attack in Syria on Friday, according to Iranian state media on Sunday. Iranian state media said the Iranian military adviser died after an Israeli attack near the Syrian capital Damascus left him wounded. The attack also killed another IRGC officer. In a tweet on Sunday, Iranian government spokesman Ali Bahadori Jahromi said the alleged Israeli attack wouldn’t go unanswered. Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman Nasser Kanaani said on Sunday that Iran has the right to respond to “state terrorism.”

China and Brazil have reached an agreement to ditch USD and use their own currencies—China Daily pic.twitter.com/q33U4kVO5Q

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

IRNA recreated a popular meme to mark China and Brazil’s decision to reportedly ditch the US dollar as an intermediary in trade, citing the Chinese state news outlet, China Daily. It shows two men representing China and Brazil posing in front of a grave labelled “USD.”

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#157. Almost 1 in 4 children live in conflict zones — report

Denial of humanitarian access, abductions and sexual violence are among several violations against children that have increased dramtically over the past year, according to a new report by Save the Children.

A record number of children were living in a conflict zone in 2025, Save the Children findings revealed on Thursday.

In the latest report in the "Stop the War on Children" series, the humantarian group said 589 million children worldwide lived in conflict zones. That's the highest number since records began in 1990, and more than double the total in the mid-1990s.

"For millions of children, fear, violence and displacement are part of every day life," said Save the Children Germany CEO Florian Westphal.

He said this needed to be a "wake-up call" for the international community.

Around two in five children in both Africa and the Middle East were living in conflict zones in 2025, the report found. Africa, alone, accounted for 44% of all children living in conflict zones worldwide.

In terms of regional proportions, 40.2% of children in the Middle East were living in conflict zones in 2025. That represented roughly two in five children across the region, and a particularly steep rise in the number of children affected. The figure jumped by nearly 29% from 52 million in 2024 to 67 million last year.

Children's exposure to conflict in Africa has more than tripled over the past 15 years, with the share of children living in conflict zones rising from 12% in 2010 to 39% in 2025.

The report highlighted the dramatic increase in the denial of humanitarian access.

A total of 8,322 verified incidents of denying access to humanitarian aid to children were recorded in 2025, a 16-fold rise over the past decade. The figure was 509 in 2015, and only eight incidents were reported when the United Nations (UN) established the Monitoring and Reporting Mechanism (MRM) in 2005.

The Palestinian Territories accounted for the vast majority of cases, with 5,946 incidents, or more than 70% of all verified cases globally.

"No grave violation has increased as dramatically or changed the overall pattern of verified violations as much as the denial of humanitarian access," the study noted.

According to the report, denial of humanitarian access rose from around 2% of all verified grave violations against children in 2015 to more than 21% in 2025, becoming the second-most frequently recorded violation after the killing and maiming of children.

Grave violations as laid out in the MRM also include the recruitment of children by armed forces, sexual violence, abduction and attacks on schools and hospitals.

The study found that, overall, 38,967 grave violations against children were verified by the UN.

This was the second-highest annual total since the MRM was established, surpassed only by the record 41,763 violations documented in 2024.

More than half of these took place in just three areas: the Palestinian Territories, the Democratic Republic of Congo and Nigeria. The Palestinian Territories alone accounted for 12,449 verified violations, nearly one-third of all cases.

As well as the denial of humanitarian access, violations in 2025 included:

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Palestinian Territories recorded the highest number of attacks on schools or hospitals, with 837 incidents, followed by Ukraine with 643. Together, the two accounted for more than half of all reported cases worldwide.

The sharpest rises in sexual violence incidents were recorded in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), where cases jumped from 358 in 2024 to 592 in 2025, and Sudan, which saw an increase from 74 to 193. Around 86% of all cases were documented in African conflict zones.

"Children have the right to live free from violence. Changing the odds requires political choices that put children’s safety first, and a sustained commitment to stop the war on children," the NGO said.

#155. Meet Phil, the bar-tailed godwit tracked on a 6,800-mile round trip to Siberia

Scientists use lightweight tracker to map wading bird’s extraordinary migration from Norfolk to the Arctic and back

A bar-tailed godwit called Phil has completed a 6,800-mile journey from Norfolk to Siberia and back again, in the first mapping of the endurance feats of this long-distance migrant.

A wading bird not much larger than a pigeon, the bar-tailed godwit is known for making the longest recorded non-stop migratory flight of any bird, with populations that visit Britain spending summers breeding in the Arctic Circle before retreating to the unfrozen wetlands of Britain in winter.

The full extent of its long-distance capabilities has been revealed thanks to RSPB scientists using a tracker weighing just 6g. This was fitted to Phil in January by the Wash Wader Research Group, which named the bird in honour of Phil Ireland, the group’s leader for many years.

Since then, the tracker has followed Phil on his journey from the Wash to the Wadden Sea coasts of the Netherlands, Denmark and Germany in March, and then on to the Taymyr peninsula in central Siberia in late May. On his successful return journey, Phil cruised over the Arctic Ocean off northern Norway, pausing on three small Norwegian islands before returning to the Wadden Sea.

He has now been recorded safely back at RSPB Snettisham on the edge of the Wash, where thousands of birds flock together at the highest of tides in one of the greatest wildlife spectacles of a British winter.

“It’s amazing to think that a bird we tagged here on the Wash in January has travelled thousands of miles to Siberia and made it all the way back again,” said Jeff Kew, a coastal adviser for the RSPB.

“By tracking Phil, we’re reminded why protecting migratory birds needs to be a global effort. Many species cross borders and depend on a whole network of wetlands along the way.”

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Bar-tailed godwits that visit Britain use part of what is known as the east Atlantic flyway, a migratory superhighway that stretches from the Arctic to South Africa and is a route for millions of birds each year. The vast mudflats of the Wash are a vital feeding and resting place for scores of species.

Dr Nigel Clark, of the Wash Wader Research Group, said: “The Wash is the UK’s single most important coastal wetland for migratory waders and spectacular numbers can be seen here in autumn and winter.

“But tracking birds like Phil the godwit shows us how connected the Wash is with the rest of the flyway. This network of sites is more important than any single ‘service station’ alone.”

#154. Tulip Siddiq says she is considering legal action against former Bangladesh leader

Former minister, who was sentenced to prison in Bangladesh in her absence, says she was victim of ‘malicious prosecution’

The former minister Tulip Siddiq has said she is considering taking legal action against the former interim leader of Bangladesh over corruption allegations made by his administration led her to resign from the UK government last year.

The MP for Hampstead and Highgate, whose aunt Sheikh Hasina was put on trial in absentia over crimes against humanity during her 15 years as prime minister of Bangladesh, was accused of benefiting from the former regime by the authorities in the Bangladeshi capital, Dhaka.

Siddiq was sentenced to two years in prison in Bangladesh in December last year after being put on trial in her absence, alongside 16 other people, over corruption allegations. She was found guilty of influencing her aunt to secure a plot of land for her family in the outskirts of Dhaka. Siddiq, who is based in London and strongly denied the charges, is unlikely to ever serve the sentence.

In an interview with the Guardian in summer 2025, Siddiq called the charges against her “completely absurd”, saying she was “collateral damage” in a longstanding feud between her aunt and Bangladesh’s former interim leader Muhammad Yunus.

Speaking to Politics Unleashed – a new podcast hosted by Guto Harri and David Davis – Siddiq said she had been the victim of a “malicious prosecution”. She added that she was speaking with a reputable lawyer who had told her she had a case to sue Yunus, the Nobel peace prize-winning economist who led the interim government in Bangladesh from 2024 to 2026.

“It was essentially malicious prosecution,” she said, adding that she was exploring options with lawyers and “considering” legal action.

Yunus, approached for a response through two close associates, declined to comment.

Siddiq said she had repeatedly tried to get details about what she was accused of, but the Bangladesh government had not replied to letters from her lawyers.

She detailed sending a handwritten letter to Yunus, who was staying at the Dorchester in London. “He refused to meet with me. I tried repeatedly to see him face to face,” she said, insisting she had not been stressed by the accusations and subsequent trial because she knew she was innocent. “I was more baffled by the whole thing than anything else,” she said.

Speaking about the impact of the trial, she compared the experience to being in a “Kafkaesque nightmare”, adding: “I had no idea what I was being convicted for. The only way I found out I had been convicted is when I woke up in the morning and saw an alert from Sky News.”

Siddiq also revealed she had received support from the former prime minister Keir Starmer during the controversy. She said he sent her a message when he was in the US with Donald Trump after she had received a security threat. “It kind of matched with the timing of when he was just about to see Trump,” she said.

Get the day’s headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

But when Starmer was asked repeatedly about Siddiq, she decided she had to resign. She said: “I had been part of the project to get Keir into No 10. I had supported him from the beginning … and then here I am, being the obstacle.”

The Starmer loyalist said the toppling of the former prime minister was “very painful”, but praised his successor, Andy Burnham.

“At the end of the day, what I want is a Labour government, another Labour government,” she said. “If Andy is going to deliver that, then I have to put my sadness aside and focus on the fact that we have a Labour government that’s doing well in the polls.”

#153. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#150. Car use in London forecast to rise by 10% in next decade

Modelling in draft of 10-year London plan threatens Sadiq Khan’s promises on improving air quality

Car use in London will rise by 10% over the next decade with cycling and walking falling as a proportion of trips, according to an official forecast by the city’s mayoralty, putting into doubt Sadiq Khan’s promises on air quality and the environment.

Based on projections in the draft version of the latest 10-year London plan, drawn up by the mayor’s office and Transport for London (TfL), road congestion from a growing population would slow buses down, while packed tube services might require stations to close regularly to ease numbers.

Khan’s office said the figures were not a prediction but modelling, based on an assumption of no new transport policies, and did not take into account the impact of recent changes such as the pedestrianisation of Oxford Street.

The expectation was that active travel levels would increase and car use would fall, they added.

The draft plan for 2026-36 sets out three possible models of growth in population and jobs, and thus demand for transport, housing and other services. The central one of these, seen as the most likely, forecasts a population of 9.9 million people, up from the current 9.1 million.

If this happens, according to the plan, the number of car or other motor vehicle trips would rise by 10% between a baseline date of 2023 and 2036, with trips using public transport going up by 17%.

Journeys by active travel, such as walking and cycling, would rise by 6%, resulting in a slight fall as a proportion of all trips taken.

A relative decline in active travel would put London at odds with the trajectory of many other European cities, for example Paris, where bike use almost tripled as a proportion of all trips in just five years.

Mayoral officials, however, dispute that this will actually happen, pointing to London’s recent growth in cycling and walking trips, with new cycle lanes and hundreds of school streets that restrict motor traffic at drop-off and pickup times.

An increase in car journeys would also bring what the report called “significant challenges” on the roads, including a possible 7% reduction in average speeds, slowing down bus routes.

On public transport, overcrowding would become more common, while on the underground, severe overcrowding at some stations “indicates the possible regular need for intervention during periods of peak demand, such as closing stations to entering passengers or nonstopping trains”, it warned.

The modelling calls into question Khan’s progress on greening London’s transport network, given his pledge in 2022 for a 27% reduction in car use across London by 2030.

Ralph Smyth, a strategy adviser for the Transport Action Network campaign group, who uncovered the projections in the plan, said progress on shifting the capital away from car use appeared to have stalled.

He said: “The new London Plan was the opportunity for the mayor to leave a legacy of a cleaner, healthier and less congested city. Inexplicably, he seems to want to turn back the clock and increase cars across the capital.”

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Khan has made progress in cleaning up London’s air, particularly with the expansion of the ultra-low emission zone to central and inner London in 2023, but the capital lags behind some other European cities and a shift towards more driving would further stall progress, even with the adoption of electric vehicles.

The transition to EVs will help, said Jenny Bates, a campaigner from Friends of the Earth, “but this will take many years – and it won’t reduce congestion”.

“London, like many cities, needs to reduce car use and reclaim space for safer walking and cycling routes, and more urban greenery to help cool the city during increasingly hot summers,” she said.

Simon Munk, the head of campaigns for the London Cycling Campaign, said the plan risked “modelling a future that is already out of date”.

He said: “TfL should not be modelling for ongoing heavy car use in London, but instead articulating how to do land-use planning and transport policy to get a lot less of it.”

A spokesperson for the mayor said: “City hall expects active travel will increase significantly by 2036 and car use will fall.

“The modelling in the London plan is based on a scenario in which no additional active travel schemes will be introduced over the next decade – which clearly is not the case, and the modelling will help identify what future transport schemes may be needed.”
